- if a model-risk pattern appears, the model may be barred from that workflow.

Risk becomes actionable, not decorative.

## 9.3 Core Risk Categories

LEXOS should recognize multiple risk categories.

At minimum, the system should classify the following:

1. Factual Risk;
2. Evidentiary Risk;
3. Legal Risk;
4. Procedural Risk;
5. Jurisdictional Risk;
6. Citation Risk;
7. Translation Risk;
8. Privilege and Confidentiality Risk;
9. Client Instruction Risk;
10. Strategy Risk;
11. Ethical Risk;
12. Reputational Risk;
13. Operational Risk;
14. Security Risk;
15. Model Risk;
16. Memory Risk;
17. Autonomy Risk;
18. Institutional Learning Risk.

Each category requires different handling.

## 9.4 Factual Risk

Factual Risk arises when material facts are uncertain, unsupported, contradicted, incomplete, stale, vague, or dependent on unreliable sources.

Examples:

- the client cannot remember a key date;
- a witness account conflicts with documents;
- a core assertion is client-confirmed only;
- the opposing file alleges facts not addressed in the client story;
- the chronology contains unexplained gaps;
- a fact has been repeated in drafts but never verified;
- a factual inference is being treated as direct proof.

Factual Risk affects:

- story development;
- evidence requests;
- strategy;
- argument drafting;
- client advice;
- and final output reliability.

### Factual Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Factual Risk through:

- truth states;
- assertion-level source links;
- contradiction objects;
- support matrices;
- client clarification requests;
- evidence-needs registers;
- W3 reconciliation for defense matters;
- W5 story-evidence alignment;
- and W9 adversarial testing.

Factual Risk should be especially visible before W6 strategy and W8 argument construction.

## 9.5 Evidentiary Risk

Evidentiary Risk arises when evidence is missing, weak, incomplete, unreliable, unauthenticated, inadmissible, poorly extracted, or misunderstood.

Examples:

- a scanned document is unreadable;
- an audio transcript has low confidence;
- a signature is unclear;
- a document appears altered;
- metadata is missing;
- chain of custody is uncertain;
- evidence supports only part of an assertion;
- a document proves that a message was sent but not that its content was true;
- evidence is privileged or cannot be used externally.

Evidentiary Risk affects whether assertions can be used in legal strategy, argument, filing, negotiation, or client communication.

### Evidentiary Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Evidentiary Risk through:

- evidence object metadata;
- extraction quality scores;
- evidence-to-assertion linkage;
- direct/indirect support classification;
- admissibility labels;
- processing logs;
- reprocessing triggers;
- human or system QA;
- and future chain-of-custody enhancements.

The MVP may use simplified evidentiary risk labels, but it must avoid treating all uploaded documents as equally reliable.

## 9.6 Legal Risk

Legal Risk arises when legal propositions, theories, claims, defenses, or interpretations are uncertain, weak, outdated, incomplete, or contradicted by authority.

Examples:

- a legal theory has no clear authority;
- adverse authority exists;
- a statute has been amended;
- precedent is distinguishable;
- an argument depends on unsettled law;
- the wrong legal standard is used;
- the burden of proof is misallocated;
- a remedy is unavailable;
- a defense is procedurally waived.

Legal Risk affects strategy, advice, drafting, and external action.

### Legal Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Legal Risk through:

- jurisdiction-specific research;
- authority hierarchy;
- citation verification;
- adverse authority search;
- W7 research loops;
- W9 adversarial legal attack;
- and legal confidence scoring.

A legal argument should not become filing-ready unless material Legal Risk has been classified and either resolved, mitigated, or expressly accepted.

## 9.7 Procedural Risk

Procedural Risk arises when deadlines, filings, service, forum rules, procedural posture, or required steps are uncertain or mishandled.

Examples:

- missed filing deadline;
- wrong court format;
- defective service;
- failure to preserve rights;
- appeal period miscalculation;
- wrong procedural vehicle;
- failure to respond to allegations;
- failure to comply with court order;
- misunderstanding hearing date or filing status.

Procedural Risk is often more dangerous than substantive weakness because procedural errors can destroy rights regardless of factual or legal merit.

### Procedural Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Procedural Risk through:

- matter calendar;
- procedural status objects;
- deadline extraction;
- court docket monitoring where available;
- jurisdictional profiles;
- workflow alerts;
- escalation rules;
- and high-severity procedural risk flags.

Procedural Risk should have low tolerance for uncertainty.

If procedural confidence is low, the system should escalate immediately.

## 9.8 Jurisdictional Risk

Jurisdictional Risk arises when the system is uncertain about the governing jurisdiction, forum, procedural law, substantive law, conflict-of-law issues, or enforceability framework.

Examples:

- multiple jurisdictions may apply;
- forum selection clause is ambiguous;
- contract law differs from procedural forum;
- foreign law evidence is required;
- privilege rules differ across jurisdictions;
- a filing is being drafted in the wrong procedural style;
- an authority from one jurisdiction is used as if binding in another.

### Jurisdictional Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Jurisdictional Risk through:

- matter-level jurisdiction fields;
- jurisdiction profiles;
- authority scope tagging;
- forum identification;
- procedural rule mapping;
- legal research scoping;
- and cross-border issue flags.

Legal research, strategy, and drafting must always be jurisdictionally scoped.

## 9.9 Citation Risk

Citation Risk arises when legal authorities are non-existent, misquoted, outdated, misapplied, overruled, irrelevant, or incorrectly cited.

This is a major risk for AI legal systems.

Examples:

- hallucinated case;
- wrong holding;
- fake quotation;
- outdated statute;
- overruled authority;
- persuasive authority presented as binding;
- incorrect citation format;
- missing pinpoint citation;
- authority from wrong jurisdiction.

### Citation Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Citation Risk through:

- citation verification;
- source retrieval;
- authority status checks;
- direct quotation validation;
- adverse treatment review;
- W7 research discipline;
- and pre-filing citation audits.

No court-facing output should be final unless citation risk is controlled.

## 9.10 Translation Risk

Translation Risk arises when language conversion changes legal meaning.

This is especially important for English/Chinese matters.

Examples:

- a legal term has no direct equivalent;
- a phrase is translated literally but not legally accurately;
- a Chinese procedural concept is rendered as a common-law concept incorrectly;
- ambiguity in the source language is removed in translation;
- speaker intent is altered;
- a translated admission becomes stronger than the original;
- dates, names, roles, or entities are mistranslated.

### Translation Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Translation Risk through:

- original text preservation;
- parallel text display;
- translation confidence;
- legal term glossary;
- ambiguity notes;
- bilingual review;
- source-linking;
- and versioned translation artifacts.

The system must not silently replace original-language records with translations.

## 9.11 Privilege and Confidentiality Risk

Privilege and Confidentiality Risk arises when sensitive material is accessed, used, disclosed, retrieved, promoted, or included in outputs improperly.

Examples:

- privileged strategy appears in a client-facing document unintentionally;
- work product enters institutional learning without sanitization;
- one matter retrieves another matter’s privileged information;
- an internal adverse assessment is sent externally;
- client admissions are overexposed;
- common-interest information is mishandled;
- vector search retrieves restricted material.

### Privilege and Confidentiality Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate this through:

- privilege labels;
- confidentiality states;
- access control;
- retrieval filtering;
- matter silos;
- output classification;
- redaction workflows;
- and institutional learning review.

Privilege risk should be treated as a structural risk, not merely a document-management issue.

## 9.12 Client Instruction Risk

Client Instruction Risk arises when the client’s instructions, authority, preferences, confirmations, or approvals are unclear, incomplete, unauthorized, inconsistent, or strategically harmful.

Examples:

- unclear settlement authority;
- instruction given by unauthorized representative;
- client denies a fact previously confirmed;
- client refuses to provide key evidence;
- client asks to make a misleading statement;
- client changes objectives;
- client authorizes action but not scope;
- client does not understand risk.

### Client Instruction Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate this through:

- structured client instruction objects;
- authority verification;
- confirmation logs;
- ambiguity flags;
- client communication records;
- approval states;
- and escalation for harmful or unclear instructions.

Autonomous action requires clear client authority.

## 9.13 Strategy Risk

Strategy Risk arises when a legal strategy is weak, premature, unsupported, overaggressive, inconsistent, too narrow, too broad, or vulnerable to opposition.

Examples:

- strategy depends on unsupported facts;
- strategy ignores adverse authority;
- argument creates a harmful admission;
- defense contradicts prior client position;
- attack line opens reputational exposure;
- strategy is legally plausible but procedurally unavailable;
- strategy optimizes short-term argument but harms long-term client position.

### Strategy Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Strategy Risk through:

- W5 support mapping;
- W6 strategy memo discipline;
- W7 research loop;
- W9 adversarial review;
- alternative strategy preservation;
- risk scoring;
- and human/system override logs.

A strategy should preserve known weaknesses and rejected alternatives.

## 9.14 Ethical Risk

Ethical Risk arises when conduct may conflict with duties of candor, confidentiality, loyalty, competence, client authority, fairness, or professional responsibility.

Even if LEXOS is designed beyond current regulatory assumptions, ethical risk remains structurally important because legal institutions must maintain legitimacy.

Examples:

- conflict of interest;
- misleading court statement;
- false evidence;
- witness coaching concern;
- unauthorized disclosure;
- improper fee handling;
- unauthorized legal action;
- client incapacity;
- concealment of adverse authority;
- misuse of confidential information.

### Ethical Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Ethical Risk through:

- conflict checks;
- privilege controls;
- candor flags;
- client authority tracking;
- evidence integrity checks;
- adverse authority detection;
- and escalation rules.

Ethical risk should be treated as a high-severity risk category.

## 9.15 Reputational Risk

Reputational Risk arises when the system’s action may harm the client, the firm, LEXOS, or associated humans/institutions beyond the immediate legal issue.

Examples:

- aggressive filing may trigger public backlash;
- weak AI-generated filing may embarrass the institution;
- client strategy may appear evasive;
- confidential information may become public;
- system error may undermine trust;
- misleading argument may damage credibility with court;
- automated client communication may seem insensitive.

### Reputational Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Reputational Risk through:

- tone review;
- external-action review;
- high-profile matter flags;
- public exposure assessment;
- strategic communications review;
- and final bundle risk classification.

Reputational risk is often not captured by pure legal analysis.

## 9.16 Operational Risk

Operational Risk arises from workflow failure, human failure, process failure, missing tasks, system outage, data inconsistency, or poor execution.

Examples:

- workflow stalls;
- evidence ingestion fails silently;
- task is not assigned;
- document version confusion;
- wrong matter ID;
- missing output;
- duplicate client record;
- incomplete handoff;
- expired pending item;
- lost client instruction;
- failed integration.

### Operational Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Operational Risk through:

- workflow state tracking;
- task queues;
- audit logs;
- exception handling;
- retry mechanisms;
- completion gates;
- orphan detection;
- deadline alerts;
- and operational dashboards.

A law firm operating system must not rely on memory or manual follow-up.

## 9.17 Security Risk

Security Risk arises from unauthorized access, data breach, credential compromise, system misconfiguration, insecure integrations, or retrieval leakage.

Examples:

- one client accesses another client’s matter;
- agent retrieves unauthorized data;
- API key exposure;
- weak access control;
- vector store leakage;
- insecure document uploads;
- malware ingestion;
- unencrypted storage;
- compromised account.

### Security Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Security Risk through:

- tenant isolation;
- matter-level access;
- role-based access;
- attribute-based access;
- encryption;
- secure secrets management;
- audit logs;
- malware scanning;
- retrieval filtering;
- and security review of integrations.

Security must be structural from MVP onward.

## 9.18 Model Risk

Model Risk arises when AI models produce incorrect, incomplete, biased, hallucinated, unstable, outdated, or poorly reasoned outputs.

Examples:

- hallucinated citations;
- incorrect legal reasoning;
- overconfident unsupported factual summary;
- poor translation;
- missed contradiction;
- weak OCR interpretation;
- model drift after provider update;
- prompt sensitivity;
- inconsistent outputs across runs.

### Model Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Model Risk through:

- model registry;
- workflow-specific model approval;
- prompt versioning;
- output verification;
- benchmark testing;
- model comparison;
- drift monitoring;
- confidence thresholds;
- and model fallback rules.

Model Risk is not solved by using stronger models.

It is solved by governance, evaluation, and verification.

## 9.19 Memory Risk

Memory Risk arises when information is stored, retrieved, promoted, demoted, or reused incorrectly.

Examples:

- matter-specific fact becomes client-level truth;
- rejected assertion resurfaces as active fact;
- privileged strategy enters institutional memory;
- old version is retrieved instead of current version;
- cross-client contamination;
- unsupported inference becomes verified memory;
- vector retrieval pulls irrelevant but semantically similar material.

### Memory Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Memory Risk through:

- memory layers;
- promotion rules;
- truth states;
- versioning;
- retrieval scoping;
- privilege labels;
- client/matter separation;
- and institutional learning governance.

Memory Risk is one of the most important risks in LEXOS because memory underlies autonomy.

## 9.20 Autonomy Risk

Autonomy Risk arises when the system acts beyond appropriate authority, proceeds without sufficient confidence, fails to escalate, or performs an action with legal effect prematurely.

Examples:

- client communication sent without sufficient review;
- external legal action taken without authority;
- workflow progresses despite critical contradiction;
- filing draft marked final too early;
- agent requests evidence in a defense matter before opposing-file reconciliation;
- system overrules risk control to complete workflow;
- autonomous action taken under wrong jurisdiction profile.

### Autonomy Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate Autonomy Risk through:

- autonomy levels;
- governance gates;
- confidence thresholds;
- action permissions;
- client authority tracking;
- workflow state controls;
- and external-action certification.

Autonomy must increase only where governance maturity supports it.

## 9.21 Institutional Learning Risk

Institutional Learning Risk arises when the system learns the wrong lessons, overgeneralizes from specific matters, absorbs privileged information, or institutionalizes errors.

Examples:

- failed argument becomes template;
- client-specific strategy becomes general playbook;
- judge-specific behavior is overgeneralized;
- privileged facts enter training memory;
- human preference is treated as legal truth;
- model error becomes accepted pattern.

### Institutional Learning Risk Controls

LEXOS should mitigate this through:

- governed learning;
- outcome classification;
- privilege screening;
- jurisdictional scope;
- abstraction;
- anonymization where necessary;
- and learning approval workflows.

Learning should improve the institution, not contaminate it.

## 9.22 Severity and Probability

Risk should be assessed by severity and probability.

Severity describes impact.

Probability describes likelihood.

A low-probability but catastrophic risk may require strong controls.

A high-probability but minor risk may be acceptable or automated.

Suggested severity levels:

1. Low;
2. Moderate;
3. High;
4. Critical;
5. Existential.

## 9.22.1 Low Risk

Minor issue with limited effect.

Example:

- formatting inconsistency;
- non-material typo;
- minor metadata gap.

## 9.22.2 Moderate Risk

Issue may affect quality or require correction but is unlikely to damage the matter materially.

Example:

- incomplete summary;
- unclear evidence label;
- moderate translation ambiguity.

## 9.22.3 High Risk

Issue may materially affect strategy, advice, evidence, argument, client understanding, or legal position.

Example:

- unsupported material fact;
- adverse authority unresolved;
- conflicting witness account.

## 9.22.4 Critical Risk

Issue may defeat a claim or defense, cause procedural loss, breach confidentiality, mislead a court, or cause serious client harm.

Example:

- missed deadline;
- false statement in filing;
- unauthorized disclosure;
- fatal factual contradiction.

## 9.22.5 Existential Risk

Issue may threaten the viability, legality, reputation, or institutional legitimacy of LEXOS itself.

Example:

- systemic cross-client data leakage;
- repeated fabricated citations in filed documents;
- uncontrolled autonomous external action;
- institutionalized false memory;
- major security breach;
- regulatory enforcement threatening system operation.

## 9.23 Risk Status

Each Risk Object should have a status.

Possible statuses include:

- identified;
- under review;
- accepted;
- mitigated;
- blocked;
- escalated;
- resolved;
- superseded;
- reopened;
- archived.

Risk status should affect workflow progression.

For example:

- accepted risk may allow continuation;
- blocked risk may prevent finalization;
- escalated risk may require review;
- resolved risk may permit workflow exit;
- reopened risk may trigger reprocessing.

## 9.24 Risk Acceptance

Not all risks can or should be eliminated.

Legal strategy often involves risk acceptance.

However, risk acceptance must be explicit.

A risk acceptance record should include:

- risk ID;
- accepting authority;
- reason for acceptance;
- alternatives considered;
- scope of acceptance;
- duration;
- affected outputs;
- and review requirement.

Autonomous systems should not silently accept risk merely to progress a workflow.

## 9.25 Risk and Workflow Control

Risk should control workflow behavior.

Examples:

- Critical Factual Risk blocks W8 finalization.
- High Citation Risk sends output back to W7.
- Procedural Risk triggers urgent escalation.
- Privilege Risk restricts retrieval.
- Memory Risk blocks promotion.
- Autonomy Risk reduces operating mode.
- Model Risk triggers secondary model validation.
- Translation Risk requires bilingual review.
- Strategy Risk triggers W9 adversarial review.
- Evidence Risk triggers W4 reprocessing or W5 support downgrade.

This creates risk-responsive autonomy.

The system becomes more autonomous when risk is low and more controlled when risk is high.

## 9.26 Risk and MVP

The MVP should implement a simplified risk framework.

Minimum MVP risk fields:

- risk type;
- risk description;
- matter ID;
- linked object;
- severity;
- status;
- mitigation note;
- created date;
- updated date.

Minimum MVP risk categories:

- factual;
- evidentiary;
- legal;
- procedural;
- citation;
- translation;
- privilege/confidentiality;
- strategy;
- operational;
- model;
- memory;
- autonomy.

Minimum MVP behavior:

- critical risks visibly block final status;
- high risks require review or acknowledgment;
- unresolved risks appear in handoffs;
- risks are linked to outputs where relevant.

This is enough for initial implementation.

The MVP does not need full probabilistic risk modeling.

But it must treat risk as structured data.

## 9.27 Target-State Risk Architecture

In target state, LEXOS should support:

- full Risk Object model;
- risk graph;
- risk-to-workflow controls;
- autonomous escalation;
- risk scoring;
- matter-level risk dashboards;
- client-level risk summaries;
- institutional risk analytics;
- model-risk monitoring;
- operational-risk monitoring;
- security-risk integration;
- and outcome-based risk calibration.

The target-state system should learn which risks were real, which were overestimated, which were missed, and which controls were effective.

## 9.28 Summary of Risk Doctrine

LEXOS must treat risk as structured institutional knowledge.

Risk should not be hidden in prose.

Risk should govern:

- memory promotion;
- workflow progression;
- output status;
- autonomy level;
- human escalation;
- external action;
- and institutional learning.

The core risk categories include:

- factual;
- evidentiary;
- legal;
- procedural;
- jurisdictional;
- citation;
- translation;
- privilege/confidentiality;
- client instruction;
- strategy;
- ethical;
- reputational;
- operational;
- security;
- model;
- memory;
- autonomy;
- and institutional learning.

The system must distinguish severity, probability, status, mitigation, and acceptance.

The objective is not to eliminate risk.

Legal work always involves risk.

The objective is to ensure that LEXOS sees risk, structures risk, acts according to risk, and learns from risk.

An autonomous legal institution that cannot govern risk cannot be trusted with autonomy.

# Section 10 — Security, Confidentiality, and Privilege Doctrine

## 10.1 Purpose of This Section

This section defines the security, confidentiality, and privilege doctrine of LEXOS.

LEXOS is designed to become an autonomous legal institution. It will handle highly sensitive legal, commercial, personal, financial, criminal, regulatory, and strategic information. The system may eventually ingest client records, court files, communications, corporate documents, evidence bundles, identity documents, financial records, legal strategies, witness information, translations, and internal risk assessments.

Because of this, security cannot be treated as a later technical enhancement.

Security must be structural.

Confidentiality cannot be treated only as a policy.

Confidentiality must be encoded into the system architecture.

Privilege cannot be treated merely as a document label.

Privilege must affect retrieval, workflow access, output generation, memory promotion, institutional learning, and external communication.

The central doctrine is:

> LEXOS must treat security, confidentiality, and privilege as architectural boundaries that govern what information may be stored, accessed, retrieved, reasoned over, promoted, disclosed, learned from, or acted upon.

This section does not define every technical security control. That belongs in the technical architecture document. This section defines the institutional doctrine that all later security engineering must follow.

## 10.2 Why Security Is More Complex in LEXOS

Security in LEXOS is more complex than ordinary application security.

A normal software system may mainly need to protect user accounts, files, databases, and application access.

LEXOS must protect legal cognition itself.

That means the system must secure not only stored documents, but also:

- extracted text;
- embeddings;
- summaries;
- assertions;
- strategy memos;
- research notes;
- translation artifacts;
- client instructions;
- evidence metadata;
- workflow logs;
- agent outputs;
- prompt context;
- model responses;
- intermediate reasoning artifacts;
- institutional learning objects;
- and retrieval results.

In an AI-native legal system, sensitive information can leak through indirect channels.

For example:

- a vector search may retrieve privileged material;
- a summary may reveal confidential strategy;
- an institutional template may preserve client-specific facts;
- a model prompt may contain sensitive data;
- an agent may use information from the wrong matter;
- a draft may include unsupported or privileged facts;
- a translation memory may expose confidential content;
- an audit log may contain sensitive excerpts;
- a research memo may reveal defense strategy.

Therefore, LEXOS security must govern the full lifecycle of legal information, not merely file access.

## 10.3 Core Security Doctrine

The core security doctrine of LEXOS is:

> No agent, workflow, model, human, retrieval process, memory layer, or output artifact should access, use, or disclose information unless it has a defined authority, scope, purpose, and permission.

This doctrine requires:

- tenant isolation;
- client isolation;
- matter isolation;
- role-based access;
- attribute-based access;
- privilege-aware retrieval;
- secure evidence handling;
- encryption;
- audit logging;
- secrets management;
- least-privilege tool access;
- secure model routing;
- and controlled institutional learning.

The system must be designed around least privilege.

Agents should not access all data merely because they are part of the system.

Workflows should not access all matters merely because they are legal workflows.

Models should not receive full context merely because they are capable of processing it.

Humans should not access every client or matter unless authorized.

Autonomy does not reduce the need for access control.

Autonomy increases it.

## 10.4 Confidentiality as a System Boundary

Confidentiality means that client and matter information must be protected from unauthorized access, misuse, or disclosure.

In LEXOS, confidentiality must apply to:

- raw uploads;
- evidence objects;
- client stories;
- matter stories;
- client instructions;
- KYC/CDD records;
- legal research;
- strategy memos;
- draft arguments;
- adversarial reports;
- final outputs;
- workflow logs;
- and institutional learning material.

Confidentiality should be represented as structured metadata.

Possible confidentiality classifications include:

- public;
- client-facing;
- internal;
- confidential;
- highly confidential;
- restricted;
- privileged;
- work product;
- court-fileable;
- external-disclosable;
- sealed;
- archived restricted;
- unknown classification.

The classification should affect:

- who may access the object;
- which agents may use it;
- which workflows may retrieve it;
- whether it can be embedded;
- whether it can be summarized;
- whether it can be included in outputs;
- whether it can be promoted;
- whether it can be learned from;
- and whether it can be exported.

Confidentiality is not only a human policy decision.

It must become operational system behavior.

## 10.5 Privilege as a Structural Control

Privilege must be treated as a structural control.

In legal systems, privilege may protect attorney-client communications, attorney work product, common-interest communications, litigation strategy, legal advice, draft filings, internal assessments, and certain confidential materials.

LEXOS must not treat privilege as a simple document tag.

Privilege may attach at multiple levels:

- document level;
- paragraph level;
- assertion level;
- communication level;
- strategy level;
- research note level;
- translation level;
- output level;
- workflow level;
- and memory layer level.

For example:

A document may contain both non-privileged factual content and privileged lawyer annotations.

A client email may contain a factual chronology and legal advice request.

A strategy memo may contain facts that are externally provable but also privileged legal analysis.

An adversarial critique may reveal internal defense weaknesses.

A translation note may reveal legal interpretation strategy.

Therefore, privilege classification should become increasingly granular over time.

The MVP may begin with simple privilege labels.

The target-state system should support fine-grained privilege boundaries.

## 10.6 Privilege States

LEXOS should support multiple privilege states.

Suggested privilege states include:

## 10.6.1 Unknown

Privilege status has not yet been determined.

Objects with unknown privilege status should be treated cautiously.

They should not be freely included in client-facing, opposing-party-facing, court-facing, or institutional-learning outputs.

## 10.6.2 Not Privileged

The object has been classified as not privileged.

This does not automatically mean it is public or externally usable. It may still be confidential.

## 10.6.3 Confidential but Not Privileged

The object is confidential but not legally privileged.

Examples:

- business records;
- financial documents;
- private correspondence;
- identity documents;
- internal company materials.

## 10.6.4 Attorney-Client Privileged

The object contains or reflects confidential legal communications between lawyer and client for the purpose of legal advice.

## 10.6.5 Attorney Work Product

The object reflects legal strategy, mental impressions, analysis, preparation, or litigation work product.

## 10.6.6 Common-Interest Privileged

The object is protected under a common-interest or joint-defense arrangement, where recognized.

## 10.6.7 Partially Privileged

The object contains both privileged and non-privileged parts.

This state requires segment-level handling or redaction.

## 10.6.8 Privilege Waived or Disclosed

The object was privileged or confidential but has been disclosed, waived, filed, or otherwise changed in status.

This does not necessarily make it freely reusable for every purpose. The scope of waiver or disclosure must be recorded.

## 10.6.9 Privilege Disputed

Privilege status is uncertain, contested, or requires legal determination.

Objects in this state should trigger restricted handling.

## 10.7 Matter Isolation

Matter isolation is mandatory.

Each legal matter must operate as a separate security and memory silo.

Matter isolation protects against:

- cross-matter privilege leakage;
- factual contamination;
- unauthorized retrieval;
- improper institutional learning;
- client conflict issues;
- strategy leakage;
- and adversarial exposure.

Every matter should have:

- matter ID;
- client ID;
- access list;
- workflow states;
- evidence repository;
- vector namespace or filtered retrieval scope;
- output repository;
- audit logs;
- and permissions.

Even where two matters involve the same client, they should not automatically share all data.

Client-level memory may be available through W1, but matter-specific memory should remain scoped.

A defense strategy from Matter A should not automatically influence Matter B.

A witness statement from Matter A should not automatically become usable in Matter B.

A privileged memo from Matter A should not be retrieved in Matter B unless the system has explicit authority.

## 10.8 Client Isolation

Client isolation is also mandatory.

LEXOS must prevent information from one client being accessed, retrieved, summarized, embedded, learned from, or disclosed in another client’s context unless expressly authorized and legally permissible.

Client isolation applies to:

- records;
- files;
- evidence;
- embeddings;
- prompts;
- outputs;
- audit logs;
- institutional learning;
- and model context.

This is especially important if LEXOS becomes multi-tenant.

A multi-tenant autonomous legal system cannot rely only on application-level separation.

It must also enforce retrieval-level, memory-level, and workflow-level isolation.

## 10.9 Tenant Isolation

If LEXOS serves multiple firms, legal teams, organizations, or external customers, tenant isolation becomes foundational.

Tenant isolation means each organization’s data, workflows, memory, models, configurations, and outputs are separated unless deliberately shared.

Tenant isolation should apply to:

- database records;
- storage buckets;
- vector indexes;
- API keys;
- model logs;
- workflow queues;
- audit logs;
- configuration;
- user accounts;
- and backups.

In early MVP, tenant isolation may be simple.

In target-state architecture, tenant isolation must be rigorous.

If LEXOS is initially used internally by one law firm or one controlled legal operation, tenant isolation may be less complex. However, the architecture should not make later multi-tenant isolation impossible.

## 10.10 Role-Based and Attribute-Based Access

LEXOS should support both role-based access control and attribute-based access control.

## 10.10.1 Role-Based Access Control

Role-based access control grants permissions based on role.

Examples of roles:

- system administrator;
- firm administrator;
- lead lawyer;
- matter lawyer;
- paralegal;
- intake specialist;
- client representative;
- accounting user;
- office admin user;
- research agent;
- evidence agent;
- drafting agent;
- adversarial agent;
- governance agent;
- viewer.

Role-based access is useful but insufficient.

## 10.10.2 Attribute-Based Access Control

Attribute-based access control grants permissions based on object and context attributes.

Relevant attributes may include:

- client ID;
- matter ID;
- jurisdiction;
- privilege status;
- confidentiality level;
- workflow stage;
- output status;
- user clearance;
- agent role;
- risk level;
- autonomy mode;
- client consent;
- legal hold status;
- and use purpose.

For example:

An Advocate agent may access filing-usable facts and evidence-linked assertions for a matter, but not raw privileged strategy memos unless permitted.

An Office Admin agent may access contact details and file-opening information but not litigation strategy.

An Accounting agent may access billing and retainer records but not evidence substance.

A Rhetorician may access final argument drafts but should not retrieve unrelated matter evidence.

This is the correct access-control direction for LEXOS.

## 10.11 Agent Access Boundaries

Agents must have access boundaries.

An agent should not have system-wide access merely because it is intelligent.

Each agent should have a defined access profile.

Examples:

## 10.11.1 Intake Specialist

May access:

- temporary intake records;
- conflict check indices;
- KYC/CDD records;
- engagement documents;
- accounting interface;
- office admin interface.

Should not access:

- unrelated matter strategy;
- unrelated client evidence;
- institutional privileged learning;
- full case files unless necessary for conflict checks under policy.

## 10.11.2 Custodian

May access:

- client-level master record;
- promoted matter facts;
- KYC/CDD refresh information;
- selected matter-level records for client-fact verification.

Should not control:

- matter strategy;
- final court arguments;
- adversarial critique outcomes.

## 10.11.3 Evidence Archivist

May access:

- uploaded evidence;
- metadata;
- extraction tools;
- matter evidence register.

Should not access:

- unrelated client matters;
- strategic memos unless required;
- final argument drafting authority.

## 10.11.4 Advocate

May access:

- supported facts;
- evidence references;
- research memos;
- strategy memos;
- argument templates.

Should not alter:

- source evidence;
- truth states;
- client master memory.

## 10.11.5 Adversary

May access:

- argument drafts;
- support matrix;
- adverse authorities;
- relevant evidence;
- opposition materials.

Should not silently modify:

- final arguments;
- evidence records;
- client facts.

The same principle applies to every agent.

Access must fit role.

## 10.12 Retrieval Security

Retrieval is one of the highest-risk areas in LEXOS.

AI systems often retrieve broadly and let the model decide relevance. This is unacceptable for legal work.

Retrieval must be permission-filtered before semantic search.

The correct retrieval sequence is:

1. Authenticate actor or agent.
2. Identify task purpose.
3. Identify permitted tenant.
4. Identify permitted client.
5. Identify permitted matter.
6. Filter by privilege and confidentiality status.
7. Filter by object type and workflow relevance.
8. Filter by truth/use status.
9. Perform semantic or keyword retrieval.
10. Return results with provenance and access reason.

The wrong retrieval sequence is:

1. Search all vectors.
2. Let the model choose what seems useful.

That design is unacceptable for LEXOS.

Semantic similarity is not legal permission.

## 10.13 Vector Store Security

Vector embeddings require special controls.

Embeddings may reveal or reconstruct sensitive information indirectly. Even where the raw text is protected, the embedding index may allow semantic leakage.

Therefore, LEXOS should treat vector stores as sensitive legal memory.

Vector security should include:

- tenant-level isolation;
- client-level or matter-level namespaces;
- metadata filtering;
- privilege-aware indexing;
- access-controlled retrieval;
- deletion and re-indexing capability;
- embedding provenance;
- and audit logs for retrieval.

The MVP may use a single pgvector database, but it should still enforce matter-level and client-level filters.

The target-state system should avoid unscoped shared vector memory.

## 10.14 Prompt and Model Context Security

When LEXOS sends information to AI models, that information becomes model context.

Model context must be governed.

The system must control:

- which data enters the prompt;
- which model receives it;
- whether the model provider stores or trains on it;
- whether data crosses jurisdictions;
- whether privileged information is included;
- whether client consent or policy permits use;
- whether redaction is needed;
- and whether output logs preserve sensitive material.

Prompt construction should be treated as a security event.

A prompt containing privileged facts is itself privileged or confidential.

Model responses may also contain confidential restatements.

Therefore, prompt and response logs must be governed like legal records.

## 10.15 Tool Access Security

Agents may use tools.

Tools may include:

- databases;
- search APIs;
- document parsers;
- OCR tools;
- email systems;
- calendar systems;
- file storage;
- billing systems;
- court databases;
- legal research systems;
- translation systems;
- document generation tools;
- and communication tools.

Tool access must follow least privilege.

An agent should have access only to tools required for its workflow.

Tool calls should be logged.

High-risk tool calls should be gated.

Examples of high-risk tool calls:

- sending client email;
- filing a document;
- deleting evidence;
- changing client master facts;
- exporting confidential files;
- sending external communications;
- updating billing records;
- modifying access permissions.

In MVP, external tool actions should be conservative.

In target-state operation, tool access can become more autonomous but must remain governed.

## 10.16 Evidence Security

Evidence requires special security handling.

Evidence may include:

- criminal defense materials;
- commercial secrets;
- financial records;
- personal data;
- communications;
- photographs;
- videos;
- witness materials;
- medical or family records;
- regulatory records;
- internal company records;
- and confidential agreements.

Evidence should be protected through:

- matter-specific storage;
- access control;
- upload validation;
- malware scanning;
- file hashing;
- extraction logs;
- original preservation;
- controlled sharing;
- and output linkage.

The MVP may implement basic digital document security.

Target-state LEXOS should support full forensic-grade evidence handling.

However, even in MVP, evidence should never be stored as an unscoped generic file.

Every evidence item should belong to a matter and have metadata.

## 10.17 Confidentiality in Outputs

Outputs can leak information.

LEXOS must classify outputs before use.

Output classifications may include:

- internal only;
- client-facing;
- court-facing;
- opposing-party-facing;
- regulator-facing;
- public;
- restricted;
- privileged;
- work product;
- draft;
- final.

A legal artifact should not move from internal to external without a status change.

Before an output becomes external, the system should check:

- intended audience;
- included facts;
- privilege status;
- confidentiality status;
- source support;
- client authority;
- jurisdictional requirements;
- and risk flags.

For example:

An adversarial memo should not become client-facing unless reviewed and reframed.

A strategy memo should not be sent to opposing counsel.

A factual summary should not include internal work product.

A court filing should not include privileged material unless intentionally waived or disclosed.

## 10.18 Redaction and Sanitization

LEXOS should support redaction and sanitization.

Redaction removes or masks sensitive information from an output.

Sanitization removes client-specific, privileged, or identifying information so material may be reused or learned from.

Redaction may apply to:

- documents;
- excerpts;
- transcripts;
- screenshots;
- research memos;
- client communications;
- court exhibits;
- and public outputs.

Sanitization may apply to:

- institutional learning;
- templates;
- model evaluation examples;
- training data;
- internal case studies;
- and reusable playbooks.

The system should preserve the relationship between original and redacted versions.

Redaction should be versioned.

Sanitization should be governed.

## 10.19 Audit Logs

LEXOS must maintain audit logs.

Audit logs should record:

- who accessed what;
- which agent accessed what;
- when access occurred;
- why access occurred;
- what workflow was involved;
- what data was retrieved;
- what tool was used;
- what output was generated;
- what object was changed;
- what permission allowed the action;
- what risk or review event occurred.

Audit logs are important for:

- security;
- accountability;
- debugging;
- legal defensibility;
- governance;
- and institutional trust.

Audit logs themselves may contain sensitive information.

Therefore, audit logs also require access controls.

## 10.20 Data Retention and Deletion

LEXOS must support retention and deletion policies.

Different data types may require different retention periods.

Examples:

- intake records;
- abandoned prospect records;
- client records;
- KYC/CDD records;
- evidence files;
- court submissions;
- billing records;
- audit logs;
- model outputs;
- operational logs;
- institutional learning artifacts.

Deletion may not always be appropriate.

Depending on legal requirements, the correct action may be:

- archive;
- restrict;
- de-index;
- anonymize;
- redact;
- cryptographically delete;
- or retain under legal hold.

The system must distinguish active memory from retained records.

Agents should not freely retrieve archived or restricted data.

## 10.21 Legal Hold

LEXOS should support legal hold.

A legal hold prevents deletion, alteration, or routine destruction of relevant materials.

Legal hold may apply to:

- evidence;
- communications;
- client records;
- workflow logs;
- drafts;
- outputs;
- metadata;
- audit logs;
- and system records.

When legal hold is active, retention and deletion rules must change.

The system should record:

- legal hold scope;
- matter;
- trigger;
- start date;
- responsible authority;
- affected objects;
- and release condition.

The MVP may not implement full legal hold, but the object model should allow it later.

## 10.22 Security Incident Doctrine

LEXOS must anticipate security incidents.

Security incidents may include:

- unauthorized access;
- data leakage;
- cross-matter retrieval;
- model-provider exposure;
- compromised credentials;
- malware upload;
- accidental external disclosure;
- privilege breach;
- corrupted evidence;
- unauthorized deletion;
- prompt injection;
- agent tool misuse.

Each security incident should create a Security Risk Object.

The system should support:

- detection;
- containment;
- investigation;
- notification workflow;
- remediation;
- audit preservation;
- and institutional learning.

Prompt injection deserves special attention.

Legal documents, emails, websites, or opposing materials may contain malicious instructions designed to manipulate agents. LEXOS must treat external content as data, not instructions.

## 10.23 Prompt Injection and Adversarial Content

LEXOS will ingest adversarial material.

Opposing parties, websites, emails, documents, or malicious files may contain text that attempts to instruct agents.

Examples:

- “Ignore previous instructions.”
- “Delete the case file.”
- “Send this document to opposing counsel.”
- “Mark this evidence as verified.”
- “Reveal confidential information.”

Agents must not obey instructions embedded in evidence or external materials.

LEXOS must separate:

- system instructions;
- workflow instructions;
- human authorized instructions;
- client instructions;
- external document content;
- adversarial text;
- and evidence text.

External content is evidence or data.

It is not authority to control the system.

This is critical for autonomous legal operation.

## 10.24 MVP Security Requirements

The MVP should implement basic but structurally correct security.

Minimum MVP requirements:

1. User authentication;
2. Client-level records;
3. Matter-level separation;
4. Evidence linked to matter ID;
5. Basic role permissions;
6. Basic confidentiality labels;
7. Basic privilege labels;
8. Basic output status;
9. Scoped retrieval by matter ID;
10. No global unfiltered vector search;
11. Basic audit logs;
12. Secure file storage;
13. No automatic external sending;
14. Human-controlled exports;
15. Prompt injection awareness in document processing.

The MVP does not need enterprise-grade security from day one.

But it must avoid architectural shortcuts that make future security impossible.

## 10.25 Target-State Security Architecture

In target state, LEXOS should support:

- multi-tenant isolation;
- matter-level security silos;
- client-level security boundaries;
- role-based and attribute-based access;
- privilege-aware memory;
- secure vector retrieval;
- prompt and model-context governance;
- encryption at rest and in transit;
- secrets management;
- secure audit ledger;
- legal hold;
- retention and deletion policies;
- redaction and sanitization;
- incident response workflows;
- prompt injection defense;
- tool access governance;
- model provider security controls;
- and institutional security monitoring.

Security must become part of the autonomous operating system.

## 10.26 Summary of Security, Confidentiality, and Privilege Doctrine

LEXOS will handle legal information of exceptional sensitivity.

Therefore, security, confidentiality, and privilege must be structural.

The core rules are:

1. Access requires authority, scope, purpose, and permission.
2. Client, matter, and tenant isolation are mandatory.
3. Privilege must affect retrieval, memory, outputs, and learning.
4. Agents must operate under least-privilege access.
5. Vector retrieval must be permission-filtered before semantic search.
6. Prompt and model context must be treated as sensitive.
7. Evidence must be matter-scoped and metadata-linked.
8. Outputs must be classified before use.
9. Audit logs must preserve accountability.
10. External content must never become system instruction.
11. MVP security may be simple, but it must preserve future security architecture.

The long-term goal is not merely to prevent unauthorized access.

The goal is to build an autonomous legal institution whose memory, reasoning, agents, workflows, and outputs are secure by design.

# Section 11 — Bilingual, Cross-Cultural, and Jurisdictional Doctrine

## 11.1 Purpose of This Section

This section defines the bilingual, cross-cultural, and jurisdictional doctrine of LEXOS.

LEXOS is designed to operate in legal environments where language, culture, jurisdiction, procedure, and legal tradition may differ. This is especially important because the system is intended to handle English/Chinese legal work and may eventually operate across civil-law, common-law, regulatory, arbitration, administrative, criminal, corporate, and cross-border contexts.

Language is not a cosmetic layer in legal work.

Jurisdiction is not a metadata field only.

Culture is not merely tone.

In legal matters, language affects facts, evidence, legal meaning, client understanding, court persuasion, procedural compliance, witness credibility, and institutional risk.

A translated word may alter the perceived meaning of an admission.

A procedural term may not have a direct equivalent across legal systems.

A business phrase may carry different implications in Taiwan, Hong Kong, Singapore, mainland China, the United States, Costa Rica, England, or other jurisdictions.

A court-facing argument that is persuasive in one legal culture may appear aggressive, vague, disrespectful, overly emotional, or insufficiently technical in another.

Therefore, LEXOS must treat bilingual and jurisdictional handling as a core legal cognition function.

The central doctrine is:

> LEXOS must preserve legal meaning across language, culture, and jurisdiction without allowing translation fluency, foreign legal analogy, or cross-cultural assumption to distort the underlying facts, law, strategy, or evidence.

## 11.2 Language Is a Legal Object

LEXOS must treat language as a legal object.

The system must not treat translation as a simple conversion from one language to another.

In legal work, language may determine:

- what was said;
- who said it;
- what was intended;
- whether a statement was an admission;
- whether a promise was made;
- whether authority was granted;
- whether a threat was communicated;
- whether a contractual obligation exists;
- whether a legal term was used correctly;
- whether a translation is court-suitable;
- and whether a client understands advice.

Therefore, language itself must be preserved, structured, and versioned.

Every important multilingual object should preserve:

- original text;
- source language;
- translated text;
- translation method;
- translator or model identity;
- translation confidence;
- legal ambiguity notes;
- term equivalence notes;
- revision history;
- approval status;
- and linked source passages.

The original text must remain authoritative unless a certified or approved translation becomes legally controlling for a defined purpose.

LEXOS must never silently replace original language with translated language.

## 11.3 Translation Is Interpretation

Translation is not mechanical substitution.

Translation is interpretation.

This is especially true when translating legal materials between English and Chinese.

A translation may require decisions about:

- legal equivalence;
- tone;
- formality;
- implied subject;
- procedural context;
- business meaning;
- cultural nuance;
- evidentiary precision;
- and rhetorical suitability.

For example, a Chinese statement may omit subjects or rely on context. An English translation may need to identify who performed an action. That identification may become legally significant.

Similarly, an English legal term may not have a direct Traditional Chinese equivalent. The translator or system may need to choose between a literal translation, a jurisdiction-specific legal translation, or an explanatory rendering.

LEXOS must preserve these translation decisions.

A translation should not merely output polished target language.

It should preserve the legal reasoning behind important translation choices.

## 11.4 Translation Layers

LEXOS should distinguish multiple translation layers.

At minimum, the system should support the following.

## 11.4.1 Raw Translation

A first-pass translation intended to make the source understandable.

This may be generated quickly by an AI model.

It is useful for triage but should not be treated as legally final.

## 11.4.2 Literal Translation

A translation that stays close to the source wording.

This is useful for evidence review, transcripts, messages, and disputed wording.

Literal translation may sound unnatural but preserves source structure.

## 11.4.3 Legal-Functional Translation

A translation that renders the legal function of the source phrase in the target language.

This is useful when terms do not map directly.

For example, a procedural term in one legal system may need to be rendered according to its closest functional equivalent in the target legal system, with a note explaining limitations.

## 11.4.4 Court-Ready Translation

A translation prepared for use in court, regulatory filing, arbitration, expert report, or formal legal submission.

This should require higher controls.

It may need human certification, translator review, bilingual legal review, or jurisdiction-specific formatting.

## 11.4.5 Client-Facing Translation

A translation designed to help the client understand legal content.

This may prioritize clarity while still preserving legal accuracy.

It should avoid oversimplification that changes meaning.

## 11.4.6 Internal Analytical Translation

A translation used for internal analysis, issue spotting, evidence mapping, or strategy.

It may include notes, alternatives, ambiguity flags, and legal interpretation commentary.

These layers should not be confused.

A raw translation should not be filed in court.

A client-facing explanation should not overwrite the original evidence text.

A legal-functional translation should preserve the reason it departed from literal wording.

## 11.5 Parallel Text Doctrine

For legally significant multilingual materials, LEXOS should preserve parallel text.

Parallel text means the system stores the original and translation together, linked line-by-line, paragraph-by-paragraph, or segment-by-segment.

This is especially important for:

- witness statements;
- WhatsApp or messaging transcripts;
- contracts;
- emails;
- court filings;
- police or prosecution materials;
- client statements;
- financial explanations;
- bilingual correspondence;
- and disputed admissions.

A parallel text record should ideally include:

- source segment ID;
- original text;
- translated text;
- translation type;
- ambiguity note;
- legal term note;
- confidence;
- reviewer;
- version;
- and approved status.

Parallel text allows downstream agents to reason from the original while using translation for accessibility.

It also allows adversarial review of translation accuracy.

## 11.6 Translation Risk

Translation Risk must be treated as a formal risk category.

Translation Risk arises when language conversion may change meaning, legal effect, tone, evidentiary value, or strategic interpretation.

Examples:

- a statement becomes stronger in translation than in the source;
- a vague source phrase becomes falsely precise;
- a legal term is translated using the wrong jurisdictional concept;
- a subject is inserted where the source was ambiguous;
- a Chinese phrase with cultural nuance is rendered too literally;
- an English contractual term is translated without preserving legal function;
- a witness statement is made to sound more certain than it was;
- a client explanation is translated in a way that appears evasive;
- a prosecutor allegation is translated as established fact.

Translation Risk should be linked to:

- source passage;
- translation artifact;
- assertion object;
- evidence object;
- output artifact;
- workflow stage;
- and legal consequence.

High Translation Risk should prevent court-ready or filing-ready status unless resolved or accepted.

## 11.7 Bilingual Evidence Handling

When evidence contains multiple languages, LEXOS must preserve the original evidentiary layer.

For bilingual evidence, the system should store:

- original file;
- extracted original text;
- translated text;
- language detection result;
- multilingual metadata;
- page and paragraph references;
- translation status;
- and legal ambiguity notes.

The evidence object should not become monolingual merely because the system generated a translation.

For example:

A Chinese email translated into English remains a Chinese source document.

The English translation is an analytical or legal artifact linked to the original.

A court-facing argument may cite the English translation, but the system must preserve source linkage to the Chinese text.

## 11.8 Cross-Cultural Meaning

LEXOS must account for cross-cultural meaning.

Legal matters often involve conduct that can be misunderstood if interpreted through the wrong cultural frame.

Examples:

- indirect business communication;
- courtesy language;
- silence or delayed response;
- non-confrontational disagreement;
- face-saving language;
- informal authority structures;
- family-business dynamics;
- verbal assurances;
- relationship-based obligations;
- intermediary roles;
- and implicit hierarchy.

The system should not automatically convert cultural patterns into legal conclusions.

For example:

Silence may not mean agreement.

Courtesy may not mean admission.

Participation in a meeting may not mean control.

Being copied on a message may not mean authorization.

Use of a title may not prove formal legal authority.

A family relationship may not prove beneficial ownership.

These are possible inferences, not facts.

LEXOS should preserve cultural interpretation as an inference or contextual hypothesis unless supported by evidence or law.

## 11.9 Jurisdiction Is a Core Matter Attribute

Every matter must have jurisdictional structure.

At minimum, each matter should identify:

- forum jurisdiction;
- governing substantive law;
- procedural law;
- court or tribunal;
- administrative agency if applicable;
- dispute type;
- language requirements;
- filing rules;
- evidence rules;
- privilege framework;
- limitation periods;
- citation style;
- enforcement jurisdiction;
- and cross-border issues.

A matter may involve multiple jurisdictions.

For example:

- client located in Taiwan;
- company incorporated in Cayman;
- contract governed by English law;
- dispute filed in Taiwan;
- evidence located in Hong Kong;
- funds transferred through Singapore;
- witnesses in Costa Rica.

LEXOS must not flatten this complexity into one jurisdiction field.

The system should support multiple jurisdictional roles.

## 11.10 Jurisdictional Roles

LEXOS should distinguish jurisdictional roles.

Possible roles include:

## 11.10.1 Forum Jurisdiction

The court, tribunal, regulator, or authority before which the matter is pending.

This determines procedure, filings, deadlines, and institutional audience.

## 11.10.2 Governing Law

The law governing the substantive legal issues.

This may differ from the forum.

## 11.10.3 Evidence Jurisdiction

The jurisdiction where evidence was created, stored, collected, authenticated, or subject to local data/evidence rules.

## 11.10.4 Enforcement Jurisdiction

The jurisdiction where judgment, award, order, or remedy must be enforced.

## 11.10.5 Client Jurisdiction

The client’s residence, incorporation, principal place of business, nationality, or regulatory home.

## 11.10.6 Counterparty Jurisdiction

The adversary’s residence, incorporation, operations, or regulatory connection.

## 11.10.7 Data Jurisdiction

The jurisdiction controlling storage, transfer, privacy, data protection, or confidentiality obligations.

## 11.10.8 Professional Responsibility Jurisdiction

The jurisdiction whose legal profession, ethics, or practice rules apply to the human or institutional legal actor.

These roles matter because one matter may involve all of them simultaneously.

## 11.11 Jurisdiction Profiles

LEXOS should eventually maintain jurisdiction profiles.

A Jurisdiction Profile is a structured knowledge object containing rules, practices, and constraints for a legal jurisdiction.

A Jurisdiction Profile may include:

- court hierarchy;
- procedural rules;
- filing requirements;
- citation standards;
- evidence rules;
- admissibility principles;
- privilege rules;
- limitation periods;
- professional responsibility rules;
- language requirements;
- court style preferences;
- common litigation patterns;
- enforcement rules;
- appellate rules;
- regulatory authorities;
- and AI-use disclosure rules where applicable.

Jurisdiction Profiles should be versioned and updated.

Legal rules change.

A jurisdiction profile must not become stale.

The MVP may begin with manually curated jurisdiction assumptions.

The target-state system should maintain structured jurisdictional intelligence.

## 11.12 Civil Law and Common Law Differences

LEXOS must account for differences between legal traditions.

Common-law systems often emphasize:

- precedent;
- adversarial briefing;
- discovery;
- cross-examination;
- judicial reasoning through prior cases;
- and advocate-driven argument.

Civil-law systems may emphasize:

- codes;
- statutes;
- judicial dossier;
- court-led procedure;
- written submissions;
- prosecutorial or judicial file structure;
- and different approaches to precedent.

Hybrid systems may combine elements of both.

The system must not assume that legal reasoning methods transfer directly.

For example:

- a common-law adversarial memo style may be inappropriate in a civil-law criminal procedure context;
- a civil-law dossier analysis may not satisfy common-law discovery strategy;
- case citation may carry different persuasive or binding value;
- witness handling may differ;
- court expectations around rhetoric may differ.

LEXOS must adapt legal cognition to jurisdictional tradition.

## 11.13 Court and Decision-Maker Culture

Beyond formal jurisdictional law, LEXOS should model decision-maker culture.

Different courts, tribunals, regulators, and judges may prefer different forms of argument.

Some may value:

- concise legal syllogism;
- procedural precision;
- evidentiary tables;
- narrative chronology;
- statutory interpretation;
- policy argument;
- commercial practicality;
- moral framing;
- technical compliance;
- or formal respect.

LEXOS should eventually learn:

- what arguments work in which forums;
- which formats are persuasive;
- which procedural missteps are punished;
- how specific judges or panels reason;
- what level of factual detail is expected;
- how visual exhibits are received;
- and which authorities are treated as persuasive.

This must be governed learning.

It should not produce stereotyping or overgeneralization.

## 11.14 Jurisdictional Scope of Legal Research

Legal research must always be scoped.

A research memo should identify:

- jurisdiction;
- forum;
- issue;
- legal question;
- authority type;
- date researched;
- source database;
- hierarchy of authority;
- adverse authority;
- and confidence.

LEXOS must prevent common AI legal research errors:

- using foreign law as if binding;
- citing outdated authority;
- mixing procedural and substantive law;
- ignoring court hierarchy;
- relying on commentary without primary authority;
- treating internet summaries as law;
- omitting adverse authority;
- and overstating persuasive authority.

A legal proposition should not become system knowledge unless its jurisdictional scope is clear.

## 11.15 Jurisdictional Risk

Jurisdictional Risk must be formalized.

Jurisdictional Risk arises when:

- forum is uncertain;
- governing law is uncertain;
- procedure is unclear;
- conflict-of-law issues exist;
- authority hierarchy is unclear;
- filing rules are unknown;
- evidence rules differ;
- privilege may not apply as assumed;
- data transfer may be restricted;
- legal advice may cross regulated boundaries;
- or external action may be unauthorized.

Jurisdictional Risk should affect workflow progression.

For example:

- W7 research should not finalize if jurisdiction is unresolved.
- W8 argument should not become court-facing if forum rules are uncertain.
- W4 evidence handling may require jurisdiction-specific data controls.
- W11 rhetorical refinement must adapt to court culture.

## 11.16 Translation and Jurisdiction Interaction

Translation and jurisdiction often interact.

A legal term may have one translation in one jurisdiction and another translation in another.

For example:

- company law terms may differ between Taiwan, Hong Kong, England, Cayman, and the United States;
- criminal procedure terms may differ between Taiwan and common-law jurisdictions;
- civil-law procedural concepts may not map into English litigation terms;
- regulatory terms may have official translations.

LEXOS should therefore avoid generic legal translation when jurisdiction-specific translation is required.

A translation should record:

- source jurisdiction;
- target jurisdiction;
- target audience;
- translation purpose;
- and whether official terminology exists.

Where official bilingual terminology exists, LEXOS should prefer it unless there is a reason not to.

Where no official equivalent exists, the system should preserve explanatory notes.

## 11.17 Multilingual Client Communications

Client communications must be language-sensitive.

The system should communicate in the client’s preferred language where appropriate, while preserving legal accuracy.

Client-facing bilingual outputs should distinguish:

- formal legal meaning;
- plain-language explanation;
- action required;
- risk warning;
- evidence request;
- deadline;
- and next step.

For important communications, LEXOS should preserve both:

- the language sent to the client;
- and an internal legal interpretation of that communication.

This helps avoid later disputes about what the client was told.

## 11.18 Multilingual Argument Drafting

When drafting arguments across languages, LEXOS must account for legal style.

A direct translation of an English legal argument into Chinese may be poor advocacy.

A direct translation of a Chinese court submission into English may sound structurally weak to common-law readers.

Therefore, multilingual argument drafting should distinguish:

1. Source-language legal analysis;
2. Target-language legal argument;
3. Jurisdiction-specific rhetorical adaptation;
4. Court or audience style;
5. Citation and authority format;
6. Formal terminology;
7. Translation notes where necessary.

W11 persuasive refinement should not merely polish language. It should ensure the output fits the legal culture and procedural audience.

## 11.19 Cross-Border Matter Doctrine

LEXOS should support cross-border matters from the start at the conceptual level, even if MVP implementation is narrow.

Cross-border matters may involve:

- multiple entities;
- foreign companies;
- offshore structures;
- different governing laws;
- cross-border evidence;
- multilingual documents;
- multiple regulators;
- foreign bank records;
- treaty or enforcement issues;
- extradition or criminal assistance;
- international arbitration;
- and parallel proceedings.

The system must identify cross-border complexity early.

A cross-border matter should trigger enhanced jurisdictional mapping.

At minimum, the system should ask:

- Which jurisdictions are involved?
- Which law governs each issue?
- Where are the parties?
- Where is the evidence?
- Where are the assets?
- Where will enforcement occur?
- Are there data-transfer concerns?
- Are there privilege conflicts?
- Are there conflicting procedural duties?
- Are translations required?
- Are foreign counsel needed?

## 11.20 Local Counsel and External Expert Integration

In cross-border or specialized matters, LEXOS may need input from local counsel or external experts.

The system should treat external expert input as structured source material.

External counsel input should be classified by:

- jurisdiction;
- issue;
- scope;
- date;
- confidence;
- authority basis;
- limitations;
- and permitted use.

External expert input should not automatically become system truth.

It should be integrated into the truth and research architecture.

The system should preserve:

- who provided the opinion;
- what assumptions it relied on;
- what jurisdiction it addressed;
- whether it was formal or informal;
- whether it was client-facing;
- and whether it has been superseded.

## 11.21 MVP Bilingual and Jurisdictional Requirements

The MVP does not need full cross-border intelligence.

However, it should preserve the correct architecture.

Minimum MVP requirements:

1. Store source language.
2. Store translated language separately.
3. Preserve original text.
4. Link translations to original segments.
5. Add translation status.
6. Add translation confidence or review status.
7. Identify matter jurisdiction.
8. Identify forum where relevant.
9. Scope legal research by jurisdiction.
10. Avoid treating foreign authority as generally applicable.
11. Support bilingual outputs where required.
12. Track translation risk where material.
13. Preserve jurisdictional assumptions in each memo.

For an English/Chinese MVP, the system should strongly support parallel text workflows.

This will be especially useful for transcripts, court files, client statements, and evidence review.

## 11.22 Target-State Bilingual and Jurisdictional Architecture

In target state, LEXOS should support:

- multilingual evidence ingestion;
- parallel text records;
- legal term glossaries;
- jurisdiction-specific translation memory;
- court-ready translation workflows;
- translation risk scoring;
- bilingual argument drafting;
- jurisdiction profiles;
- cross-border issue mapping;
- authority hierarchy by jurisdiction;
- procedural rule engines;
- data jurisdiction controls;
- local counsel integration;
- court culture learning;
- and multilingual institutional memory.

This architecture should allow LEXOS to operate across jurisdictions without collapsing legal differences into generic legal summaries.

## 11.23 Summary of Bilingual, Cross-Cultural, and Jurisdictional Doctrine

LEXOS must treat language, culture, and jurisdiction as core legal cognition elements.

The governing rules are:

1. Original language must be preserved.
2. Translation must be treated as interpretation.
3. Parallel text should be used for important materials.
4. Translation confidence and ambiguity must be tracked.
5. Legal terms must be jurisdictionally aware.
6. Client-facing translations must preserve legal accuracy.
7. Court-facing translations require higher controls.
8. Jurisdiction must be structured, not assumed.
9. Legal research must be jurisdiction-scoped.
10. Civil-law and common-law reasoning differences must be respected.
11. Cross-border matters require explicit jurisdictional mapping.
12. Culture may inform interpretation but must not become unsupported fact.
13. The MVP may begin simply, but it must preserve source language, translation linkage, and jurisdictional scope.

The long-term objective is to create an autonomous legal institution capable of reasoning accurately across languages, legal systems, legal cultures, and jurisdictions without losing factual precision, legal meaning, or evidentiary discipline.

# Section 12 — Evidence, Provenance, and Legal Artifact Doctrine

## 12.1 Purpose of This Section

This section defines the evidence, provenance, and legal artifact doctrine of LEXOS.

LEXOS is designed to become an autonomous legal institution. It must therefore be able to receive, classify, interpret, preserve, cite, challenge, and reuse legal materials without losing their source identity or evidentiary meaning.

Legal work is not built on text alone.

It is built on records.

Those records may include:

- client statements;
- contracts;
- court filings;
- prosecution files;
- correspondence;
- invoices;
- corporate records;
- bank statements;
- registry records;
- screenshots;
- photographs;
- audio;
- video;
- transcripts;
- expert reports;
- internal notes;
- legal research;
- strategy memos;
- argument drafts;
- translations;
- and final filings.

LEXOS must understand the difference between:

- evidence;
- source material;
- extracted text;
- structured metadata;
- factual assertions;
- legal conclusions;
- strategic interpretations;
- advocacy language;
- and final legal artifacts.

The central doctrine is:

> Every legal output generated by LEXOS must be traceable to structured sources, evidence, reasoning, authority, workflow origin, and version history.

This doctrine applies even when the MVP begins with simple digital document ingestion.

The MVP may simplify forensic rigor.

It must not simplify provenance into nonexistence.

## Evidence Ingestion Doctrine

LEXOS treats evidence ingestion as foundational institutional infrastructure—not an optional enrichment pass added after cognition is built.

The system must preserve **original evidence** while producing **high-quality derived extraction artifacts**, including markdown, structured JSON, transcripts, OCR text, visual descriptions, metadata manifests, embeddings (when retrieval is enabled), and summarized views for operators.

Derived extraction artifacts support search, assertion mapping, support matrices, drafting, and agent cognition, but **they do not replace** the preserved original file. Extraction quality, uncertainty, QA flags, and human review obligations must remain visible across workflows, UI surfaces, retrieval layers, and governance gates.

## 12.2 Evidence as a Structured Legal Object

Evidence must be treated as a structured legal object, not merely as an uploaded file.

An uploaded file becomes legally useful only when it is classified, linked, processed, and made available for controlled reasoning.

An Evidence Object should include, at minimum:

- evidence ID;
- matter ID;
- client ID;
- file name;
- file type;
- evidence type;
- source or uploader;
- upload timestamp;
- original file reference;
- extracted text reference;
- extracted structured data reference;
- metadata;
- language;
- processing status;
- extraction confidence;
- linked assertions;
- linked issues;
- linked workflows;
- confidentiality status;
- privilege status;
- version;
- and audit history.

In the target-state architecture, the Evidence Object may also include:

- cryptographic hash;
- chain-of-custody events;
- forensic metadata;
- authenticity status;
- admissibility status;
- redaction status;
- exhibit number;
- court-use status;
- and provenance graph links.

The MVP does not need all fields operationally.

But the object model should anticipate them.

## 12.3 Source Material vs Evidence

LEXOS must distinguish source material from evidence.

Source material is any input that may contain legally relevant information.

Evidence is source material that has been identified, classified, and linked to a matter or assertion as potentially supporting, contradicting, or contextualizing a legal position.

Examples of source material:

- intake notes;
- public website;
- news article;
- client email;
- social media post;
- draft document;
- internal memo;
- registry extract;
- transcript;
- bank record;
- court pleading.

Not all source material becomes evidence.

For example:

- a website may be used for background research but not evidence;
- an internal AI summary may be a work product but not evidence;
- a lawyer note may support internal analysis but not court proof;
- a client oral statement may guide investigation but require corroboration.

LEXOS should classify whether source material is:

- background source;
- evidentiary source;
- legal authority;
- client instruction;
- internal work product;
- research source;
- translation source;
- operational record;
- or institutional learning material.

This prevents the system from using every input as if it were evidence.

## 12.4 Evidence Does Not Equal Meaning

Evidence does not interpret itself.

An Evidence Object may contain text, images, numbers, signatures, metadata, timestamps, or communications. But the legal meaning of that evidence must be expressed through Assertions.

For example:

A bank statement may support the assertion:

> A transfer of USD 50,000 occurred on 14 June 2023.

It may not, by itself, support the assertion:

> The transfer was fraudulent.

An email may support the assertion:

> A message was sent from Person A to Person B.

It may not prove:

> Person B read and accepted the content.

A company registry record may support:

> Person X was not formally listed as a director.

It may not prove:

> Person X exercised no de facto control.

Therefore, LEXOS must preserve the distinction between:

- Evidence Object;
- Evidentiary Assertion;
- Factual Assertion;
- Legal Inference;
- Strategic Interpretation;
- and Argument Node.

This distinction is essential to prevent evidence overreading.

## 12.5 Evidence Support Types

LEXOS should classify how evidence supports an assertion.

At minimum, support types should include:

## 12.5.1 Direct Support

The evidence directly establishes the assertion.

Example:

- a signed contract directly supports that the contract was signed;
- a bank record directly supports that a transfer occurred;
- a court docket directly supports that a filing occurred.

## 12.5.2 Indirect Support

The evidence supports the assertion indirectly through context or inference.

Example:

- a sequence of emails indirectly supports that a meeting was planned;
- transaction timing indirectly supports a relationship between payments;
- a message thread indirectly supports knowledge or awareness.

Indirect support must be clearly labeled.

## 12.5.3 Partial Support

The evidence supports part of the assertion but not all of it.

Example:

- a document shows a meeting occurred but not what was said;
- a receipt shows payment but not purpose;
- a message shows contact but not authorization.

## 12.5.4 Contradictory Evidence

The evidence contradicts the assertion.

Example:

- client claims not to have received a document, but email logs show delivery;
- opposing file alleges one date, but metadata shows another;
- a witness statement conflicts with bank records.

## 12.5.5 Contextual Evidence

The evidence provides context but does not directly prove or disprove the assertion.

Example:

- industry background;
- corporate group chart;
- historical correspondence;
- regulatory environment.

## 12.5.6 Negative Evidence

The absence of expected evidence is treated as relevant.

Example:

- no registry record exists;
- no email appears in expected period;
- no signature is present;
- no payment appears in bank records.

Negative evidence must be used cautiously.

Absence of evidence does not always prove absence of fact.

## 12.6 Provenance Doctrine

Provenance means the system can explain where information came from and how it moved through LEXOS.

Provenance should exist for:

- documents;
- evidence;
- assertions;
- research;
- translations;
- strategy;
- arguments;
- outputs;
- memory promotions;
- and final legal artifacts.

A provenance record should answer:

- What is the source?
- Who or what introduced it?
- When was it introduced?
- Which workflow processed it?
