- Which agent processed it?
- Which model processed it?
- What transformations occurred?
- What version is current?
- What objects rely on it?
- What outputs used it?
- Has it been challenged, corrected, superseded, or withdrawn?

Provenance is not merely technical metadata.

In legal work, provenance is part of defensibility.

## 12.7 Transformation Provenance

LEXOS must track transformations.

A raw document may become:

- uploaded file;
- extracted markdown;
- extracted JSON;
- translated text;
- summarized evidence item;
- linked assertion;
- support matrix entry;
- argument citation;
- visual exhibit;
- final filing reference.

Each transformation should be traceable.

Transformation provenance should record:

- input object;
- output object;
- transformation type;
- tool or model used;
- timestamp;
- confidence;
- quality flags;
- reviewer if any;
- and version.

This is especially important for OCR, translation, summarization, and legal drafting.

The system must avoid treating transformed material as identical to the original.

A summary is not the source.

A translation is not the source.

An extraction is not the source.

A visual exhibit is not the source.

They are derived artifacts.

## 12.8 Legal Artifact Doctrine

A Legal Artifact is any structured output created by LEXOS for legal, operational, evidentiary, strategic, client-facing, or institutional use.

Examples include:

- Client Onboarding File;
- Client Master Story File;
- Case Master Story File;
- Evidence Register;
- Reconciliation Report;
- Support Matrix;
- Client Gap Report;
- Research Memo;
- Strategy Memo;
- Argument Draft;
- Adversarial Challenge Memo;
- Visual Exhibit;
- Translation Bundle;
- Client Update;
- Court Filing Draft;
- Final Presentation Bundle;
- Institutional Learning Note.

A Legal Artifact must have metadata.

At minimum:

- artifact ID;
- artifact type;
- client ID;
- matter ID where applicable;
- workflow origin;
- authoring agent or human;
- source objects;
- version;
- status;
- intended audience;
- confidentiality status;
- privilege status;
- truth dependencies;
- review state;
- creation timestamp;
- last modified timestamp;
- and supersession status.

This prevents output sprawl.

It also prevents drafts from being mistaken for final materials.

## 12.9 Artifact Status

Every Legal Artifact should carry status.

Suggested statuses include:

- working draft;
- internal draft;
- pending review;
- returned for revision;
- approved internal;
- client-facing draft;
- client-facing final;
- court-facing draft;
- filing-ready;
- externally sent;
- filed;
- superseded;
- withdrawn;
- archived.

Artifact status should control use.

For example:

- a working draft should not be sent externally;
- an internal adversarial memo should not become client-facing automatically;
- a court-facing draft should not be filed unless filing-ready;
- a superseded report should not be used as current truth;
- a withdrawn artifact should remain auditable but inactive.

A filename cannot determine legal status.

The system status controls the artifact.

## 12.10 Artifact Audience

LEXOS should classify the intended audience of each artifact.

Possible audiences include:

- internal system;
- internal legal team;
- human supervising lawyer;
- client;
- court;
- tribunal;
- regulator;
- opposing party;
- expert witness;
- accounting;
- office administration;
- public;
- institutional learning layer.

Audience affects:

- tone;
- privilege;
- confidentiality;
- level of detail;
- legal risk;
- citation requirements;
- translation standard;
- review requirement;
- and finality threshold.

For example:

An internal strategy memo may include candid weaknesses.

A client update may include risk but in controlled language.

A court filing must be precise, supported, and procedurally compliant.

An institutional learning note must avoid client-identifying confidential details unless permitted.

## 12.11 Artifact Dependency Mapping

Legal Artifacts should know what they depend on.

Dependency mapping allows LEXOS to determine whether an artifact must be updated when upstream information changes.

Example:

A W8 argument may depend on:

- Case Master Story version 3;
- Support Matrix version 2;
- Evidence Objects E-12, E-18, E-24;
- Research Memo R-5;
- Strategy Memo S-3;
- Authority Objects A-7 and A-9.

If Evidence Object E-18 is later marked unreliable, the system should identify all artifacts that relied on it.

If Research Memo R-5 is superseded, arguments depending on it may require review.

If a translated passage is corrected, outputs using the prior translation may be affected.

Dependency mapping is essential for autonomous maintenance.

## 12.12 Exhibit Doctrine

An Exhibit is an Evidence Object or derived evidentiary artifact prepared for legal use.

Not every Evidence Object is automatically an Exhibit.

An uploaded file may become an Evidence Object.

An Evidence Object may later be selected, numbered, organized, translated, redacted, or formatted as an Exhibit.

Exhibit status should include:

- exhibit ID;
- evidence source;
- matter ID;
- exhibit title;
- exhibit description;
- source file;
- relevant pages;
- relevant passages;
- translation status;
- redaction status;
- admissibility status if known;
- court-use status;
- and linked argument sections.

The MVP may use simple exhibit IDs.

The target-state system should support full exhibit lifecycle.

## 12.13 Citation Doctrine

Citations connect legal artifacts to sources.

LEXOS should support several citation types:

1. Evidence citation;
2. Legal authority citation;
3. Procedural record citation;
4. Client instruction citation;
5. Research memo citation;
6. Translation citation;
7. Internal artifact citation.

Each citation should identify:

- source object;
- pinpoint reference where possible;
- citation type;
- citation purpose;
- authority or evidence status;
- confidence;
- and verification state.

Court-facing citations require higher confidence than internal citations.

A citation should not exist merely as text.

It should ideally link to a Source or Evidence Object.

## 12.14 Evidence Citation vs Legal Citation

LEXOS must distinguish evidence citations from legal citations.

Evidence citations support factual assertions.

Legal citations support legal propositions.

Example evidence citation:

> Exhibit E-14, Bank Statement, p. 3, transfer entry dated 14 June 2023.

Example legal citation:

> Article X of statute Y; Case Z; regulation section A.

A legal argument often requires both.

The system must not use legal authority to support factual assertions or evidence to support legal propositions without proper reasoning.

## 12.15 Source Reliability

LEXOS should classify source reliability.

Source reliability may depend on:

- source type;
- authenticity;
- independence;
- completeness;
- recency;
- official status;
- internal consistency;
- external corroboration;
- and context.

Suggested source reliability classes:

- authoritative official source;
- authenticated original source;
- verified internal source;
- reliable external source;
- client-provided source;
- opposing-party source;
- informal source;
- AI-generated derivative source;
- unknown reliability source;
- unreliable source.

Source reliability should influence truth state and confidence.

## 12.16 Extraction Quality

For parsed documents, OCR, transcripts, and structured extraction, LEXOS should track extraction quality.

Extraction quality may include:

- text confidence;
- layout confidence;
- table extraction confidence;
- handwriting confidence;
- speaker identification confidence;
- timestamp confidence;
- language detection confidence;
- page completeness;
- missing sections;
- unreadable areas;
- processing warnings.

An extraction with low confidence should not be treated as fully reliable.

The system may need to trigger:

- reprocessing;
- human review;
- alternative model processing;
- manual correction;
- or quality downgrade.

## 12.17 Original Preservation

LEXOS must preserve originals or original digital surrogates.

Derived artifacts are not substitutes.

The system should preserve:

- original upload;
- original filename;
- original metadata where available;
- upload context;
- hash where implemented;
- source notes;
- and link to all derived versions.

For physical documents, the MVP may only preserve digital surrogates.

Target-state LEXOS should support stronger chain-of-custody records.

But even MVP should avoid losing the original uploaded file.

## 12.18 Chain-of-Custody Doctrine

Target-state LEXOS should support chain-of-custody tracking.

Chain of custody records who had control of evidence, when, and what happened to it.

This is especially important for criminal defense, regulatory matters, fraud disputes, contested documents, and high-stakes litigation.

A chain-of-custody record may include:

- original holder;
- transfer event;
- receiving person or system;
- timestamp;
- method of transfer;
- storage location;
- processing event;
- hash;
- alteration or redaction event;
- and certification.

The MVP may defer full chain-of-custody.

However, the Evidence Object schema should anticipate it.

At minimum, MVP should record uploader, upload time, file reference, and matter linkage.

## 12.19 Authenticity and Admissibility

Evidence may be authentic, relevant, and still inadmissible.

Evidence may be admissible for one purpose but not another.

LEXOS must distinguish:

- authenticity;
- relevance;
- admissibility;
- weight;
- completeness;
- privilege;
- and strategic usefulness.

For example:

A screenshot may be relevant but require authentication.

A hearsay statement may be useful for investigation but not admissible for truth.

A privileged email may be accurate but unusable externally.

A document may be admissible but strategically harmful.

The MVP may not implement detailed admissibility analysis.

Target-state LEXOS should support jurisdiction-specific admissibility classification.

## 12.20 Visual Artifacts

Visual artifacts are legal artifacts.

They must be source-linked.

Examples:

- timelines;
- transaction diagrams;
- ownership charts;
- relationship maps;
- issue matrices;
- damages tables;
- procedural timelines;
- evidence heatmaps.

A visual artifact should include:

- source data;
- evidence links;
- methodology;
- assumptions;
- generated file;
- version;
- intended audience;
- review status;
- and caption text.

Visuals can be persuasive but dangerous.

A misleading visual can distort the record.

Therefore, W10 outputs must remain tied to evidence and argument sections.

## 12.21 Drafts and Finality

LEXOS must preserve the distinction between drafts and final artifacts.

Drafts may contain:

- tentative language;
- unresolved assertions;
- incomplete citations;
- unverified law;
- alternative arguments;
- internal comments;
- or placeholders.

Final artifacts require stronger controls.

Finality should require:

- status transition;
- source check;
- risk check;
- version lock;
- review or governance event;
- and audience classification.

A document should not become final by being exported.

It becomes final only when system conditions are met.

## 12.22 Supersession and Withdrawal

Legal artifacts may be superseded or withdrawn.

A superseded artifact has been replaced by a newer version.

A withdrawn artifact should no longer be used.

Supersession and withdrawal should not delete history.

The system should record:

- superseding artifact;
- reason;
- date;
- affected outputs;
- downstream dependency warnings;
- and whether external correction is needed.

This is important when:

- evidence changes;
- translations are corrected;
- citations are invalidated;
- strategy changes;
- client instructions change;
- court filings are amended;
- or risk status changes.

## 12.23 Provenance and Institutional Learning

Institutional learning must preserve provenance.

LEXOS should not learn abstract patterns without knowing where they came from.

For example:

A lesson such as “jurisdiction argument type X succeeded” should preserve:

- jurisdiction;
- procedural posture;
- facts;
- court;
- decision date;
- strategy used;
- opposing argument;
- outcome;
- limitations;
- and whether client-specific details were removed.

A lesson without provenance may become dangerous overgeneralization.

Institutional learning objects should never expose confidential client facts unless properly authorized and classified.

## 12.24 MVP Evidence and Artifact Requirements

The MVP should implement simplified but correct evidence and artifact handling.

Minimum MVP requirements:

1. Upload digital documents.
2. Assign evidence ID.
3. Link evidence to matter ID and client ID.
4. Store original file.
5. Extract text or markdown.
6. Store basic metadata.
7. Classify evidence type.
8. Track processing status.
9. Allow evidence-to-assertion linkage.
10. Allow basic citation to evidence.
11. Version major outputs.
12. Classify artifact status.
13. Preserve source links for legal drafts.
14. Record basic provenance.
15. Prevent unsupported final outputs where visible.

The MVP does not need full forensic chain-of-custody, advanced admissibility, automated authenticity scoring, or court-grade exhibit preparation.

But it must preserve the concept that evidence is a structured object and outputs are legal artifacts.

## 12.25 Target-State Evidence and Artifact Architecture

In target state, LEXOS should support:

- all-media evidence ingestion;
- forensic metadata;
- chain-of-custody records;
- cryptographic hashing;
- authenticity review;
- admissibility classification;
- evidence graph;
- assertion-level evidence support;
- exhibit lifecycle;
- legal citation engine;
- artifact registry;
- dependency mapping;
- versioned outputs;
- redaction and sanitization;
- visual artifact provenance;
- and finality certification.

This architecture should allow LEXOS to explain and defend every final legal artifact it produces.

## 12.26 Summary of Evidence, Provenance, and Legal Artifact Doctrine

LEXOS must treat evidence, provenance, and artifacts as core legal infrastructure.

The governing rules are:

1. Evidence is a structured legal object, not merely a file.
2. Source material and evidence must be distinguished.
3. Evidence does not interpret itself.
4. Assertions express what evidence supports, contradicts, or contextualizes.
5. Provenance must be preserved across transformations.
6. Derived artifacts must remain linked to original sources.
7. Legal artifacts require metadata, status, audience, and versioning.
8. Citations should link to structured source objects.
9. Visuals must be evidence-linked.
10. Finality must be a system status, not a filename.
11. Superseded and withdrawn artifacts must remain auditable.
12. MVP may simplify forensic rigor, but not evidence structure.
13. Target-state LEXOS should support full evidentiary defensibility.

The long-term goal is that every final output produced by LEXOS can be traced back through its facts, evidence, sources, legal authorities, reasoning steps, workflow stages, agents, models, and versions.

That traceability is what makes autonomous legal work defensible.

# Section 13 — Agent Doctrine and Role Architecture

## 13.1 Purpose of This Section

This section defines the agent doctrine of LEXOS.

LEXOS uses agents to execute legal, operational, evidentiary, strategic, research, administrative, and governance functions. However, agents must not be misunderstood as the system itself.

Agents are functional actors within the broader autonomous legal institution.

The durable intelligence of LEXOS resides in:

- its institutional doctrine;
- object model;
- memory architecture;
- truth model;
- provenance system;
- workflow architecture;
- governance rules;
- risk controls;
- and institutional learning layer.

Agents execute within that architecture.

This distinction is critical because a poorly designed agent system can become chaotic, duplicative, over-permissive, inconsistent, or unsafe. A strong agent system must define roles, authority, memory access, tool permissions, output obligations, workflow boundaries, escalation duties, and accountability.

The central doctrine is:

> LEXOS agents are governed legal cognition modules. They may act autonomously within defined authority, but they must remain subordinate to institutional memory, truth states, provenance, workflow gates, and system governance.

## 13.2 Agents Are Not Lawyers

LEXOS may contain agents that perform work traditionally done by lawyers.

Agents may:

- interview clients;
- analyze facts;
- review evidence;
- conduct legal research;
- draft arguments;
- identify strategy;
- challenge legal theories;
- prepare client reports;
- generate court-facing materials;
- and refine persuasive writing.

However, agents should not be conceptually modeled as individual human lawyers.

Human lawyers are biological professionals with personal judgment, licensure, ethical duties, emotions, reputational concerns, and subjective memory.

LEXOS agents are role-constrained cognitive modules.

This matters because agent behavior should not depend on simulated personality alone.

An agent should be defined by:

- function;
- permissions;
- inputs;
- outputs;
- tools;
- memory access;
- workflow scope;
- risk duties;
- escalation rules;
- and prohibited actions.

An agent is not credible because it “sounds like” a lawyer.

It is credible because it operates inside a legally disciplined architecture.

## 13.3 Agents as Institutional Roles

Agents should be understood as institutional roles.

A role exists because the system needs a recurring function.

For example:

- Intake Specialist exists because onboarding requires screening, conflict review, KYC/CDD, engagement coordination, and handoff.
- Custodian exists because the client master record requires persistent stewardship.
- Story Architect exists because case narratives must be developed, tested, confirmed, and versioned.
- Evidence Archivist exists because evidence must be ingested, structured, and catalogued.
- Analyst exists because assertions must be mapped to evidence.
- Strategist exists because legal positions must be organized into attack or defense lines.
- Librarian exists because research must be scoped, sourced, verified, and linked.
- Advocate exists because arguments must be constructed from facts, evidence, and law.
- Adversary exists because the system must attack its own work.
- Visualizer exists because complex evidence may need visual expression.
- Rhetorician exists because final work product must be clear, persuasive, and audience-appropriate.

These roles may initially be implemented as named agents.

In the target-state system, they may become dynamically composed reasoning modules.

The role is more important than the agent identity.

## 13.4 Static Agents vs Dynamic Agentic Configurations

The initial LEXOS workflow model uses named agents. This is useful for design, implementation, explanation, and early orchestration.

However, the target-state architecture should not permanently depend on rigid static agents.

Over time, LEXOS may evolve toward dynamic agentic configurations.

A dynamic agentic configuration is a temporary cognitive assembly created for a specific task.

For example, to draft a jurisdiction argument, LEXOS may assemble:

- a factual analyst;
- a jurisdiction researcher;
- a citation verifier;
- an adversarial critic;
- a drafting module;
- a translation module;
- and a governance checker.

Once the task is complete, that configuration may dissolve.

This is more flexible than maintaining fixed agents for every possible function.

However, even dynamic configurations must follow governance rules.

They must still have:

- task scope;
- memory access limits;
- output requirements;
- tool permissions;
- and validation obligations.

## 13.5 Agent Authority

Each agent must have defined authority.

Agent authority determines what the agent may do without additional approval.

Authority should be defined along several dimensions:

1. Data access authority;
2. Tool authority;
3. Object creation authority;
4. Object modification authority;
5. Workflow transition authority;
6. Communication authority;
7. External action authority;
8. Memory promotion authority;
9. Risk escalation authority;
10. Finalization authority.

No agent should have unlimited authority.

Even a senior reasoning agent should not be able to access all data, change all records, send all communications, or finalize all outputs.

Authority must follow function.

## 13.6 Data Access Authority

Data access authority defines what an agent may read.

Examples:

An Intake Specialist may read:

- intake records;
- conflict indices;
- KYC materials;
- engagement templates;
- accounting setup status;
- office admin setup status.

The Intake Specialist should not read unrelated matter strategy or evidence except as needed for conflict analysis under strict policy.

A Custodian may read:

- client master record;
- client facts;
- client story;
- promoted matter facts;
- KYC updates;
- selected matter materials where needed for verification.

The Custodian should not freely read all matter strategy.

An Advocate may read:

- supported facts;
- evidence citations;
- strategy memos;
- research memos;
- authority lists;
- argument templates.

The Advocate should not freely read unrelated matters or modify evidence records.

An Adversary may read:

- argument drafts;
- support matrix;
- relevant evidence;
- adverse authority;
- opposing file;
- strategic weaknesses.

The Adversary should access enough to attack effectively but should not overwrite the work it critiques.

## 13.7 Tool Authority

Tool authority defines what tools an agent may use.

Tools may include:

- database queries;
- vector retrieval;
- file storage;
- evidence parsing;
- OCR;
- transcription;
- legal research APIs;
- web search;
- translation tools;
- document generation;
- email;
- calendar;
- billing systems;
- court portals;
- filing systems;
- and communication channels.

High-risk tools require stronger controls.

Examples of high-risk tools:

- sending email to client;
- sending email to opposing counsel;
- filing court documents;
- deleting records;
- changing access permissions;
- approving payment records;
- modifying client master facts;
- exporting evidence;
- updating legal status;
- initiating external action.

The MVP should restrict high-risk tools heavily.

Target-state LEXOS may allow more tool autonomy, but only with strong governance.

## 13.8 Object Creation Authority

Agents may create objects.

Examples:

- Intake Specialist creates Intake Records and Onboarding Files.
- Custodian creates Client Fact records and Promotion Review records.
- Story Architect creates Case Story Assertions and Case Master Story artifacts.
- Evidence Archivist creates Evidence Objects.
- Analyst creates Support Matrix entries.
- Librarian creates Research Memos and Authority Objects.
- Strategist creates Strategy Objects.
- Advocate creates Argument Nodes and Draft Artifacts.
- Adversary creates Challenge Objects and Risk Objects.
- Visualizer creates Visual Artifacts.
- Rhetorician creates Final Refinement Artifacts.

Object creation must follow schema.

Agents should not create unstructured records where structured objects are required.

## 13.9 Object Modification Authority

Object modification authority is more sensitive than object creation authority.

An agent may be allowed to create a draft object but not modify authoritative records.

For example:

- W8 Advocate may draft an argument but may not change the underlying Evidence Object.
- W11 Rhetorician may revise language but may not change fact truth states.
- W7 Librarian may update research confidence but may not promote client facts into W1.
- W3 Story Architect may revise the Case Master Story but should not silently change W2 history.
- W1 Custodian may update Client Facts but only through promotion and verification rules.

Modification authority should be explicit.

Critical records should require versioning.

No significant legal object should be silently mutated.

## 13.10 Workflow Transition Authority

Some agents may move workflows forward.

For example:

- Evidence Archivist may mark W4 extraction complete;
- Analyst may mark support mapping ready for strategy;
- Strategist may send memos to research;
- Advocate may send argument draft to adversarial review;
- Adversary may return challenges to Advocate;
- Rhetorician may prepare final bundle for certification.

Workflow transitions should be permitted only when exit conditions are met.

An agent should not move a workflow forward merely because it has produced text.

Workflow transition should require:

- required objects;
- required status fields;
- risk checks;
- unresolved issue checks;
- and governance validation.

## 13.11 Communication Authority

Communication authority defines whether an agent may communicate with humans or external parties.

Communication types include:

- internal system messages;
- internal team updates;
- client questions;
- client reports;
- evidence requests;
- billing-related messages;
- opposing counsel communications;
- court communications;
- regulator communications;
- public communications.

Client and external communications are high-risk.

The MVP should generally require human approval for external communications.

Target-state LEXOS may support autonomous communication under defined categories, such as:

- routine evidence reminders;
- factual confirmation requests;
- status updates;
- document upload requests;
- scheduling messages;
- low-risk administrative communications.

High-risk communications should require stronger certification.

Examples:

- settlement proposal;
- admission;
- court filing;
- legal advice;
- risk warning;
- waiver discussion;
- opposing counsel position letter.

## 13.12 External Action Authority

External action authority is the highest-risk authority.

External actions include:

- filing documents;
- sending demand letters;
- submitting regulatory responses;
- contacting courts;
- communicating with opposing parties;
- making settlement offers;
- transmitting evidence;
- signing or executing legal documents;
- sending client advice with legal effect.

LEXOS target-state may eventually support external action authority.

However, this authority should require:

- client authority;
- jurisdictional compatibility;
- output finalization;
- risk review;
- evidence support;
- citation verification;
- privilege review;
- and action logging.

External action should never be a casual tool call.

## 13.13 Memory Promotion Authority

Memory promotion authority defines whether an agent may move information from one memory layer to another.

This is one of the most dangerous agent authorities.

Examples:

- W2 may propose promotion of client-level facts to W1.
- W3 may propose promotion of client facts discovered during reconciliation.
- W5 may propose promotion of evidence-supported facts.
- W1 controls final client-level memory promotion.
- Institutional learning agents may propose abstracted learning objects.

Most agents should propose promotion, not directly execute it.

The Custodian or governance layer should control high-impact promotions.

This prevents matter-specific or unsupported information from becoming cross-matter institutional truth.

## 13.14 Risk Escalation Authority

Agents should be able to escalate risk.

Risk escalation is not merely a human-supervision feature. It is part of autonomous governance.

An agent should escalate when it detects:

- unsupported material fact;
- critical contradiction;
- missing evidence;
- adverse legal authority;
- citation uncertainty;
- privilege concern;
- translation ambiguity;
- procedural deadline risk;
- client instruction ambiguity;
- model uncertainty;
- security concern;
- or autonomy boundary issue.

Escalation may route to:

- another workflow;
- governance agent;
- human reviewer;
- system administrator;
- client clarification queue;
- or risk register.

An agent that cannot escalate uncertainty is unsafe.

## 13.15 Finalization Authority

Finalization authority defines whether an agent may mark an output as final.

Finalization should be rare.

Most agents should produce drafts or intermediate artifacts.

Finalization should require governance conditions.

For example:

A W11 Rhetorician may prepare a final presentation bundle, but finalization should require:

- no unresolved critical factual risk;
- no unresolved citation risk;
- adversarial loop closed;
- output status verified;
- source dependencies intact;
- privilege classification complete;
- and required human or system approval.

In MVP, finalization should usually require human review.

In target-state, finalization may become autonomous for defined output classes.

## 13.16 Core LEXOS Agent Roles

The current LEXOS doctrine includes the following core agent roles.

## 13.16.1 Intake Specialist

Primary workflow:

- W0.

Core function:

- conduct client onboarding, screening, KYC/CDD, conflict checks, engagement preparation, and handoff.

Primary outputs:

- Intake Record;
- Client Onboarding File;
- KYC/CDD Status;
- Engagement Package;
- Draft Master Record for W1.

Key constraints:

- ephemeral operational memory;
- no permanent matter strategy role;
- no uncontrolled access to active case files;
- no final client acceptance without required governance or sign-off in the applicable operating mode.

## 13.16.2 Custodian

Primary workflow:

- W1.

Core function:

- maintain the persistent client-level memory layer.

Primary outputs:

- Client Master Record;
- Client Facts;
- Client Master Story;
- Client Fact Audit Log;
- Fact Promotion Decisions;
- KYC Refresh Records.

Key constraints:

- does not replace matter strategy;
- must control client-level memory promotion;
- must preserve verification ladder;
- must prevent cross-matter contamination.

## 13.16.3 Story Architect

Primary workflows:

- W2;
- W3.

Core function:

- develop, confirm, test, and reconcile case narratives.

Primary outputs:

- Case Master Story File;
- Client Confirmation Log;
- Reconciliation Reports;
- Evidence-Needs Register;
- Case-Fact Audit Log;
- Fact Promotion Requests.

Key constraints:

- must distinguish client narrative from verified fact;
- must not demand defense-side evidence before W3 where W3 is required;
- must preserve contradictions and vulnerabilities;
- must version story changes.

## 13.16.4 Intake Clerk

Primary workflow:

- W3.

Core function:

- ingest, index, and structure opposing case files.

Primary outputs:

- Structured Opposing-Case Docket;
- Paragraph-Level Index;
- Filing Timeline;
- Extracted Filings;
- Metadata Records.

Key constraints:

- must preserve paragraph-level source fidelity;
- must not convert allegations into verified facts;
- must support reconciliation, not strategy finalization.

## 13.16.5 Evidence Archivist

Primary workflow:

- W4.

Core function:

- ingest, normalize, extract, catalogue, and embed evidence.

Primary outputs:

- Evidence Objects;
- Markdown Extracts;
- JSON Extracts;
- Metadata Manifests;
- Exhibit IDs;
- Processing Logs.

Key constraints:

- does not determine final legal significance;
- must preserve original source;
- must log extraction quality;
- must link evidence to matter and evidence-needs register.

## 13.16.6 Analyst

Primary workflow:

- W5.

Core function:

- map assertions to evidence and classify support.

Primary outputs:

- Support Matrix;
- Unsupported Fact List;
- Contradiction List;
- Client Gap Report;
- Pending Testimony Register;
- Fact Promotion Requests.

Key constraints:

- must not overstate support;
- must distinguish direct, indirect, partial, and contradictory evidence;
- must preserve unsupported assertions;
- must route unresolved gaps appropriately.

## 13.16.7 Strategist

Primary workflow:

- W6.

Core function:

- generate legal strategy from supported facts, risks, and legal theories.

Primary outputs:

- Strategy Point List;
- Strategy Memos;
- Attack or Defense Lines;
- Risk-Weighted Strategy Map.

Key constraints:

- strategy must remain linked to support matrix;
- unsupported-fact dependency must remain visible;
- alternatives and rejected strategies should be preserved.

## 13.16.8 Librarian

Primary workflows:

- W5 research support;
- W7.

Core function:

- conduct legal and factual research, source grading, authority review, and research memo production.

Primary outputs:

- Research Memos;
- Authority Objects;
- Adverse Authority Lists;
- Citation Confidence Records;
- Research Compendium.

Key constraints:

- must jurisdictionally scope research;
- must verify authorities;
- must identify adverse authority;
- must not invent citations;
- must distinguish primary authority, secondary authority, commentary, and background sources.

## 13.16.9 Advocate

Primary workflow:

- W8.

Core function:

- construct legal arguments from strategy, facts, evidence, and research.

Primary outputs:

- Argument Nodes;
- Argument Drafts;
- Allegation-Response Matrix;
- Fact Schedule;
- Legal Argument Bundle.

Key constraints:

- must not create unsupported facts;
- must link material claims to evidence or authority;
- must not alter source records;
- must preserve weakness indicators where relevant.

## 13.16.10 Adversary

Primary workflow:

- W9.

Core function:

- attack arguments, strategies, facts, evidence, and legal positions from opposing perspectives.

Primary outputs:

- Adversarial Challenge Memo;
- Attack Matrix;
- Weakness Register;
- Revision Checklist;
- Loop Exit Recommendation.

Key constraints:

- must not be deferential to W8;
- must identify credible attacks;
- must preserve unresolved weaknesses;
- must not silently rewrite arguments.

## 13.16.11 Visualizer

Primary workflow:

- W10.

Core function:

- convert complex legal and evidentiary material into visual artifacts.

Primary outputs:

- Timelines;
- Flowcharts;
- Entity Maps;
- Transaction Diagrams;
- Evidence Tables;
- Visual Exhibit Pack.

Key constraints:

- must link visuals to source evidence;
- must preserve assumptions;
- must avoid misleading simplification;
- must classify audience and use status.

## 13.16.12 Rhetorician

Primary workflow:

- W11.

Core function:

- refine final writing and visuals for clarity, persuasion, coherence, and audience fit.

Primary outputs:

- Final Presentation Bundle;
- Refined Briefs;
- Client-Ready or Court-Ready Versions;
- Rhetorical Change Log.

Key constraints:

- must not invent facts;
- must not weaken evidentiary precision;
- must not erase caveats required by truth state;
- must not override upstream support classification.

## 13.16.13 Accounting Agent

Primary workflow:

- W0 and operational finance interfaces.

Core function:

- handle retainer status, billing identifiers, fee mapping, payment terms, and financial setup.

Primary outputs:

- Retainer Status;
- Billing Record;
- Finance Metadata;
- Payment Confirmation.

Key constraints:

- should access only financial and administrative data necessary for its role;
- should not access legal strategy or evidence unless strictly required.

## 13.16.14 Office Admin Agent

Primary workflow:

- W0 and matter administration.

Core function:

- coordinate file opening, team assignment, access control, contact details, and administrative setup.

Primary outputs:

- File Opening Record;
- Matter Access Setup;
- Team Roster;
- Administrative Checklist.

Key constraints:

- should access administrative data, not full legal strategy;
- must apply access controls correctly.

## 13.17 Governance Agents

Target-state LEXOS should include governance agents.

Governance agents monitor, restrict, audit, or certify other agents and workflows.

Possible governance agents include:

## 13.17.1 Epistemic Integrity Auditor

Reviews truth states, source support, contradiction handling, and unsupported assertions.

## 13.17.2 Privilege Boundary Controller

Monitors privilege and confidentiality risks across retrieval, outputs, and learning.

## 13.17.3 Citation Verification Controller

Verifies that cited legal authorities exist, are current, and support the proposition.

## 13.17.4 Jurisdictional Compliance Controller

Ensures that research, procedure, filings, and arguments are jurisdictionally scoped.

## 13.17.5 Workflow Gatekeeper

Determines whether workflow entry and exit conditions are satisfied.

## 13.17.6 Memory Promotion Auditor

Reviews proposed promotions between matter, client, and institutional memory.

## 13.17.7 Model Risk Auditor

Tracks model performance, hallucination patterns, drift, and workflow suitability.

## 13.17.8 Output Certification Agent

Reviews final artifacts for source support, status, privilege, risk, and readiness.

These agents may be implemented later.

In MVP, these functions may be simplified or partially manual.

However, the architecture should anticipate them.

## 13.18 Agent Collaboration

Agents must collaborate through structured artifacts, not informal conversation alone.

An agent handoff should include:

- source agent;
- target agent;
- workflow;
- matter ID;
- relevant objects;
- artifact versions;
- unresolved risks;
- open questions;
- required next action;
- and handoff timestamp.

For example:

The Analyst should not simply tell the Strategist, “the evidence looks good.”

The Analyst should hand over:

- support matrix;
- supported facts;
- unsupported facts;
- contradiction list;
- evidence references;
- pending testimony;
- confidence status;
- and open risks.

Structured collaboration prevents ambiguity.

## 13.19 Agent Disagreement

Agents will disagree.

Disagreement is valuable.

Examples:

- Advocate believes an argument is strong; Adversary finds it vulnerable.
- Strategist prioritizes a defense; Librarian finds adverse law.
- Analyst marks a fact partially supported; Story Architect believes the client confirmation is sufficient.
- Rhetorician wants stronger language; Epistemic Auditor blocks overstatement.
- Custodian rejects a fact promotion from a matter workflow.

Disagreement should not be erased.

It should be structured.

The system should record:

- agents involved;
- issue in dispute;
- competing positions;
- supporting sources;
- confidence levels;
- risk effect;
- resolution;
- and whether dissent remains preserved.

In autonomous systems, dissent is a safety mechanism.

## 13.20 Agent Evaluation

Agents must be evaluated.

Evaluation should not be based only on user satisfaction.

Agent quality should be measured by:

- factual accuracy;
- citation accuracy;
- evidence-linking quality;
- contradiction detection;
- legal reasoning quality;
- jurisdictional discipline;
- translation accuracy;
- risk detection;
- output usefulness;
- compliance with workflow role;
- and downstream correction rate.

For example:

A Rhetorician may produce elegant prose but fail if it erases uncertainty.

An Advocate may produce persuasive arguments but fail if citations are weak.

A Librarian may produce long research memos but fail if adverse authority is missed.

An Evidence Archivist may process many documents but fail if extraction quality is poor.

Evaluation must be role-specific.

## 13.21 Agent Memory

Agents should not possess unrestricted personal memory.

The institution should own memory.

Agents may access memory according to role and task.

This prevents:

- hidden agent-specific knowledge;
- inconsistent context;
- cross-matter contamination;
- untracked reasoning;
- and agent lock-in.

If an agent learns something important, it should be stored as a proper object in the appropriate memory layer.

The system should not depend on an agent “remembering” privately.

## 13.22 Agent Replacement and Upgrade

Agents should be replaceable.

A mature LEXOS architecture should allow:

- model upgrades;
- prompt changes;
- role splitting;
- role merging;
- tool replacement;
- performance tuning;
- and new agent introduction.

Replacing an agent should not destroy institutional memory.

This is another reason memory must belong to the institution, not the agent.

Agent replacement should preserve:

- role definition;
- object schemas;
- workflow contracts;
- output requirements;
- and governance rules.

## 13.23 Agent Failure Modes

LEXOS must anticipate agent failure modes.

Common failure modes include:

## 13.23.1 Role Drift

Agent begins performing functions outside its role.

Example:

- Rhetorician adds new facts;
- Advocate changes evidence status;
- Evidence Archivist makes legal conclusions.

## 13.23.2 Overconfidence

Agent presents uncertain conclusions as certain.

## 13.23.3 Hallucination

Agent invents facts, citations, sources, or procedural rules.

## 13.23.4 Context Leakage

Agent uses information from the wrong client, matter, or memory layer.

## 13.23.5 Goal Misalignment

Agent optimizes for output completion rather than legal correctness.

## 13.23.6 Confirmation Bias

Agent reinforces the client’s preferred narrative without adequate testing.

## 13.23.7 Adversarial Weakness

Agent fails to identify how the opposition will attack.

## 13.23.8 Tool Misuse

Agent calls tools incorrectly, excessively, or without authority.

## 13.23.9 Silent Mutation

Agent changes authoritative records without proper versioning.

## 13.23.10 Security Boundary Violation

Agent accesses or discloses restricted material.

Each failure mode should map to governance controls.

## 13.24 MVP Agent Architecture

The MVP should implement a small number of agents or agentic workflows.

It does not need the full target-state agent ecosystem.

A practical MVP may include:

1. Case Story Agent;
2. Evidence Ingest Agent;
3. Fact Support Agent;
4. Research Agent;
5. Strategy/Argument Agent;
6. Adversarial Review Agent;
7. Basic Governance Checker.

These may correspond to W2, W4, W5, W7, W8, W9, and a minimal control layer.

The MVP should not overbuild agent complexity.

It should prove the core loop:

- story;
- evidence;
- support;
- research;
- argument;
- adversarial critique;
- versioned output.

Agent roles can be refined later.

## 13.25 Target-State Agent Architecture

In target state, LEXOS should support:

- specialized workflow agents;
- dynamic agentic configurations;
- governance agents;
- model-specific subagents;
- jurisdiction-specific agents;
- language-specific agents;
- evidence-specific agents;
- adversarial panels;
- autonomous arbitration;
- and institutional learning agents.

However, all agents must remain subordinate to:

- object model;
- memory architecture;
- governance rules;
- risk doctrine;
- and provenance system.

The agent layer should be powerful but replaceable.

## 13.26 Summary of Agent Doctrine

LEXOS agents are governed legal cognition modules.

They are not the institution itself.

The system must define each agent by:

- role;
- purpose;
- workflow;
- inputs;
- outputs;
- tools;
- memory access;
- authority;
- prohibited actions;
- escalation duties;
- and evaluation criteria.

The major rules are:

1. Agents must operate inside institutional governance.
2. Agents must have limited authority.
3. Agents must access only permitted memory.
4. Agents must use structured artifacts for collaboration.
5. Agents must preserve truth state and provenance.
6. Agents must not silently mutate authoritative records.
7. Agents must escalate risk and uncertainty.
8. Agents may disagree, and dissent should be preserved.
9. Agents should be replaceable.
10. Institutional memory belongs to LEXOS, not to individual agents.
11. MVP should use a small, practical agent set.
12. Target-state LEXOS may evolve toward dynamic agentic configurations and governance agents.

The long-term objective is not to create many impressive agents.

The objective is to create a governed autonomous legal institution in which agents perform specialized legal cognition reliably, safely, and coherently.

# Section 14 — Model, Tool, and Automation Doctrine

## 14.1 Purpose of This Section

This section defines the doctrine for AI models, tools, automations, and execution systems within LEXOS.

LEXOS is an autonomous legal cognition institution, not a single AI model. It may use many models, tools, parsers, databases, retrieval systems, workflow engines, communication systems, document systems, and automation services. These components are necessary, but they are not the institution itself.

The institution is defined by its doctrine, object model, memory architecture, truth model, workflows, governance rules, risk controls, and legal cognition architecture.

Models and tools are replaceable components inside that architecture.

This distinction matters because AI systems often fail when model capability is mistaken for system reliability. A powerful model can still hallucinate, misread evidence, invent citations, ignore jurisdictional limits, overstate confidence, leak context, or produce persuasive but unsupported conclusions.

Likewise, tools can create risk if agents use them without authority, context, permission, or logging.

The central doctrine is:

> LEXOS must treat AI models and tools as governed execution resources, not as sources of institutional truth or autonomous authority.

Models may reason.

Tools may act.

But neither model output nor tool execution should bypass truth state, provenance, memory boundaries, risk controls, or workflow governance.

## 14.2 LEXOS Is Model-Agnostic but Model-Aware

LEXOS should be model-agnostic at the architectural level.

This means the system should not depend permanently on one provider, one model family, one prompt style, one embedding model, one OCR model, or one agent framework.

Models will change.

Some models will improve.

Some will degrade.

Some will become unavailable.

Some will become too expensive.

Some will become unsuitable for confidentiality reasons.

Some will perform better in English than Chinese.

Some will reason well but cite poorly.

Some will extract documents well but draft badly.

Some will be strong at adversarial critique but weak at structured output.

Therefore, LEXOS should be able to route tasks to different models based on function, risk, language, cost, confidentiality, jurisdiction, and required reliability.

However, model-agnostic does not mean model-indifferent.

LEXOS must be model-aware.

It must know which model is being used, for what purpose, under which configuration, with what limitations, and with what verification requirements.

## 14.3 Model Output Is Not Truth

A model output is not truth.

A model output is a generated artifact or reasoning proposal.

It may be useful, persuasive, accurate, insightful, or strategically valuable. But it does not become a verified fact, valid legal authority, confirmed translation, or final legal conclusion merely because a model produced it.

LEXOS must classify model outputs according to their function.

A model output may be:

- extraction result;
- summary;
- draft;
- hypothesis;
- inference;
- classification;
- translation;
- research synthesis;
- strategic recommendation;
- adversarial critique;
- risk flag;
- legal argument;
- or final artifact candidate.

Each type has different reliability requirements.

For example:

- a document summary must trace to source text;
- a translation must trace to original language;
- a legal citation must be verified against source authority;
- a factual inference must be labeled as inference;
- a strategy recommendation must identify assumptions and risks;
- a final argument must link to facts, evidence, and legal authority.

The system must never treat model fluency as evidence.

## 14.4 Model Registry

LEXOS should maintain a Model Registry.

The Model Registry records which models are approved, for what tasks, under what conditions.

Each model entry should include:

- model name;
- provider;
- version;
- deployment mode;
- context window;
- input/output modality;
- supported languages;
- known strengths;
- known weaknesses;
- approved workflows;
- prohibited workflows;
- confidentiality rating;
- cost profile;
- latency profile;
- benchmark results;
- last evaluation date;
- drift observations;
- and retirement status.

The Model Registry allows LEXOS to govern model use systematically.

Without a Model Registry, model selection becomes ad hoc, and legal reliability becomes difficult to audit.

## 14.5 Model Task Suitability

Different legal tasks require different model capabilities.

LEXOS should classify model suitability by task type.

Examples:

## 14.5.1 Intake Conversation

Requires:

- bilingual fluency;
- structured questioning;
- sensitivity to client narrative;
- ability to collect missing data;
- ability to avoid giving premature legal conclusions;
- and ability to escalate risk.

## 14.5.2 Document Extraction

Requires:

- OCR quality;
- layout preservation;
- table extraction;
- handwriting or stamp recognition where relevant;
- language detection;
- and output structure discipline.

## 14.5.3 Fact Analysis

Requires:

- ability to compare assertions;
- identify inconsistencies;
- preserve uncertainty;
- distinguish allegation from fact;
- and avoid narrative overcompression.

## 14.5.4 Legal Research

Requires:

- source discipline;
- jurisdictional awareness;
- ability to identify adverse authority;
- ability to distinguish binding from persuasive sources;
- and ability to avoid hallucinated authorities.

## 14.5.5 Legal Drafting

Requires:

- long-form coherence;
- legal structure;
- evidence citation discipline;
- rhetorical control;
- jurisdictional style;
- and ability to preserve caveats.

## 14.5.6 Adversarial Review

Requires:

- ability to attack assumptions;
- identify weak evidence;
- generate opposing theories;
- detect overstatement;
- and resist agreement bias.

## 14.5.7 Translation

Requires:

- bilingual precision;
- legal terminology awareness;
- preservation of ambiguity;
- ability to distinguish literal and legal-functional translation;
- and source linkage.

No model should be assumed suitable for all tasks.

## 14.6 Model Risk

Model Risk is the risk that a model produces outputs that are wrong, incomplete, misleading, unstable, biased, hallucinated, insecure, overconfident, or unsuitable for the legal context.

Model Risk may arise from:

- hallucinated facts;
- hallucinated citations;
- faulty legal reasoning;
- weak jurisdictional awareness;
- overconfident drafting;
- poor bilingual interpretation;
- inaccurate extraction;
- context loss;
- prompt sensitivity;
- model drift;
- provider changes;
- training-data limitations;
- refusal or over-compliance behavior;
- hidden biases;
- or inability to preserve structured status labels.

LEXOS must treat Model Risk as a formal risk category.

A model should be trusted only for defined tasks under defined controls.

## 14.7 Model Governance Controls

LEXOS should govern model use through multiple controls.

## 14.7.1 Approved Use Cases

Each model should have approved use cases.

For example:

- Model A may be approved for long-form drafting but not citation verification.
- Model B may be approved for Chinese/English translation but not strategy.
- Model C may be approved for OCR review but not legal conclusions.
- Model D may be approved for adversarial critique but not client communication.

## 14.7.2 Prohibited Use Cases

Some models should be expressly prohibited from certain tasks.

Examples:

- a non-confidential model should not receive privileged client facts;
- a weak citation model should not create final legal research;
- a low-cost model should not finalize high-risk legal arguments;
- an extraction model should not make legal admissibility conclusions.

## 14.7.3 Configuration Control

Model settings should be controlled.

Relevant configuration includes:

- temperature;
- system prompt;
- tool access;
- context scope;
- retrieval scope;
- output format;
- citation requirement;
- confidence requirement;
- and safety/guardrail settings.

A model used for legal extraction may need deterministic settings.

A model used for adversarial brainstorming may need broader exploratory behavior.

Configuration should match purpose.

## 14.7.4 Prompt Versioning

Prompts are part of the legal system.

A prompt can affect output quality, bias, risk, and reliability.

LEXOS should version important prompts.

Prompt records should include:

- prompt ID;
- workflow;
- agent role;
- model;
- version;
- purpose;
- input requirements;
- output schema;
- constraints;
- date activated;
- author;
- change history;
- and evaluation notes.

A legal artifact should ideally be traceable to the prompt version that generated it.

## 14.7.5 Output Verification

Model outputs should be verified according to risk.

Examples:

- extracted text should be checked against source quality;
- legal citations should be verified against actual authorities;
- factual summaries should be compared to evidence;
- translations should preserve source meaning;
- strategy outputs should be adversarially reviewed;
- final drafts should be checked for unsupported assertions.

Verification may be done by another model, a deterministic tool, a governance agent, or a human.

## 14.7.6 Model Comparison

For high-risk tasks, LEXOS may compare outputs across multiple models.

Multi-model comparison may be useful for:

- legal issue spotting;
- adversarial review;
- translation;
- source extraction;
- argument risk;
- and citation checking.

However, model consensus is not proof.

Multiple models may share the same error.

Consensus should increase confidence only where outputs are independently grounded.

## 14.7.7 Drift Monitoring

Model performance may change over time.

LEXOS should monitor drift.

Examples of drift:

- citation accuracy declines;
- translation style changes;
- extraction quality changes;
- model becomes more verbose;
- model becomes more deferential;
- model begins omitting caveats;
- model provider silently changes behavior.

Drift monitoring may use benchmark tasks, regression suites, human audits, and output analytics.

## 14.8 Tool Doctrine

Tools allow LEXOS to perceive, retrieve, process, transform, communicate, and act.

Tools may include:

- databases;
- document parsers;
- OCR systems;
- transcription systems;
- vector databases;
- search APIs;
- legal research systems;
- court databases;
- file storage;
- calendar systems;
- email systems;
- billing systems;
- identity verification systems;
- e-signature systems;
- document generation systems;
- visualization tools;
- and workflow orchestrators.

Tools expand agent capability, but they also create risk.

The doctrine is:

> Tools must be permissioned, scoped, logged, and purpose-bound.

No agent should use a tool unless the workflow and role permit it.

## 14.9 Tool Registry

LEXOS should maintain a Tool Registry.

Each tool entry should include:

- tool name;
- tool type;
- provider;
- purpose;
- approved workflows;
- approved agents;
- required permissions;
- data sensitivity rating;
- external data transfer status;
- logging behavior;
- failure modes;
- cost profile;
- reliability notes;
- and security review status.

The Tool Registry prevents uncontrolled tool sprawl.

## 14.10 Tool Authority

Tool Authority determines which agents may use which tools and for what purpose.

Examples:

- Evidence Archivist may use document parsing and OCR tools.
- Librarian may use legal research tools.
- Advocate may use document generation tools.
- Visualizer may use charting and diagram tools.
- Accounting Agent may use billing tools.
- Office Admin Agent may use access-control and file-opening tools.
- Governance Agent may use audit and policy-check tools.

High-risk tools should require stricter authority.

High-risk tools include:

- external email;
- court filing portals;
- client notification systems;
- deletion tools;
- billing action tools;
- access control tools;
- external publication tools;
- and evidence export tools.

## 14.11 Tool Calls as Auditable Events

Every significant tool call should be auditable.

A tool call record should include:

- tool used;
- calling agent;
- workflow;
- matter ID;
- client ID if applicable;
- purpose;
- input parameters;
- output summary;
- timestamp;
- success/failure status;
- error message if any;
- and resulting object changes.

For sensitive tools, logs should preserve enough information to audit without unnecessarily exposing privileged content.

Tool logs themselves may be confidential.

## 14.12 Tool Failure Doctrine

Tools will fail.

LEXOS must expect failure.

Tool failures may include:

- OCR failure;
- file parsing error;
- API outage;
- search failure;
- database timeout;
- corrupted upload;
- failed embedding;
- failed email delivery;
- legal research service unavailable;
- billing system mismatch;
- calendar sync failure;
- document generation error.

Tool failure should not silently disappear.

Each failure should create:

- failure record;
- affected workflow;
- affected object;
- retry status;
- risk classification;
- escalation requirement;
- and resolution status.

For example:

If evidence extraction fails, W4 should not mark the evidence complete.

If citation lookup fails, W7 should not treat the authority as verified.

If email sending fails, the client communication should not be assumed delivered.

## 14.13 Automation Doctrine

Automation is the execution of tasks or workflows without continuous human intervention.

LEXOS should automate aggressively where automation improves speed, consistency, reliability, and institutional memory.

But automation must be governed.

Automation should be appropriate for:

- routine classification;
- metadata extraction;
- document parsing;
- evidence cataloging;
- task creation;
- deadline reminders;
- workflow handoffs;
- support matrix updates;
- research queue generation;
- version tracking;
- contradiction scanning;
- and internal reports.

Automation should be cautious for:

- legal conclusions;
- external communications;
- client advice;
- settlement authority;
- court filings;
- privilege determinations;
- adverse ethical issues;
- and high-risk strategic decisions.

The target state may automate even high-risk functions, but only after governance matures.

## 14.14 Workflow Orchestration

LEXOS requires workflow orchestration.

Workflow orchestration coordinates:

- task sequence;
- agent assignment;
- tool calls;
- state transitions;
- data dependencies;
- retry logic;
- failure handling;
- human review points;
- governance checks;
- and handoffs.

The orchestration system should know:

- which workflow is active;
- which stage is complete;
- what inputs are missing;
- what agents are assigned;
- what outputs are required;
- what risks are unresolved;
- what dependencies exist;
- and what can happen next.

Without orchestration, agents become disconnected task performers.

With orchestration, LEXOS becomes an operating system.

## 14.15 Deterministic Systems and AI Systems

LEXOS should distinguish deterministic systems from AI systems.

Deterministic systems produce predictable outputs according to rules.

Examples:

- database constraints;
- access-control checks;
- status transitions;
- date calculations;
- hash generation;
- file storage;
- schema validation;
- citation format validation;
- workflow gate checks.

AI systems produce probabilistic outputs.

Examples:

- summarization;
- translation;
- issue spotting;
- strategy generation;
- adversarial critique;
- legal drafting.

LEXOS should use deterministic systems wherever rules are clear.

AI should not be used where deterministic validation is available and superior.

Examples:

- use deterministic checks for required fields;
- use deterministic date calculations for deadlines where rules are coded;
- use deterministic schema validation for outputs;
- use deterministic access-control filters before semantic retrieval;
- use deterministic citation existence checks where possible.

AI should perform cognition, not replace basic control logic.

## 14.16 Structured Outputs

Agents and models should produce structured outputs where possible.

Structured outputs may include:

- JSON;
- markdown with fixed headings;
- database-ready records;
- tables;
- object updates;
- risk entries;
- assertion records;
- evidence links;
- citation lists;
- contradiction objects;
- workflow status updates.

Structured output reduces ambiguity and supports automation.

Freeform prose may be useful for final legal writing, client communication, and narrative explanation. But internal system operation should prefer structured outputs.

For example:

A W5 support analysis should not only say:

> The evidence mostly supports the story.

It should produce:

- assertion ID;
- assertion text;
- support status;
- supporting evidence IDs;
- contradiction IDs;
- confidence;
- gap note;
- next action.

## 14.17 Schema Discipline

LEXOS should enforce schema discipline.

Schema discipline means agents must output information according to defined object structures.

This is essential for:

- database integrity;
- workflow automation;
- retrieval;
- audit;
- analytics;
- and downstream reasoning.

A schema should define:

- required fields;
- optional fields;
- allowed values;
- validation rules;
- object relationships;
- status values;
- and lifecycle states.

Schemas should be versioned.

If schemas change, the system should know which records use which schema version.

## 14.18 Model-Tool Interaction

Models may call tools.

This increases capability but also risk.

Tool-using models must be governed by:

- allowed tool list;
- input constraints;
- permission checks;
- tool-use logging;
- output validation;
- failure handling;
- and post-call reasoning review.

A model should not be able to use a tool merely because the tool exists.

The workflow must authorize tool use.

For example:

A Research Agent may use legal research tools for W7.

It should not use email tools.

An Evidence Archivist may use OCR tools.

It should not use court filing tools.

A Governance Agent may read audit logs.

It should not alter evidence unless specifically authorized.

## 14.19 External Search Doctrine

External search may be useful for:

- public records;
- legal updates;
- background information;
- adverse media;
- sanctions;
- corporate registries;
- court records;
- factual research;
- jurisdictional research;
- and source discovery.

However, external search results are not automatically reliable.

LEXOS should classify external sources by:

- source type;
- authority;
- date;
- jurisdiction;
- relevance;
- reliability;
- and verification status.

Search snippets should not become facts.

Search results should be followed to source documents where possible.

External search should be logged when it influences legal work.

## 14.20 Legal Research Tool Doctrine

Legal research tools require special handling.

LEXOS should prefer authoritative sources for legal propositions.

Legal research tools should support:

- statutes;
- regulations;
- case law;
- official court materials;
- administrative guidance;
- secondary commentary;
- citation history;
- treatment status;
- and jurisdiction filters.

The system should distinguish:

- primary authority;
- secondary authority;
- commentary;
- practical guidance;
- news;
- summaries;
- AI-generated legal explanations.

A legal research output should never rely solely on an AI summary when the underlying authority is available.

## 14.21 Embedding and Retrieval Tool Doctrine

Embedding and retrieval tools support semantic search.

They are useful for:

- evidence retrieval;
- case story support;
- legal research retrieval;
- prior output recall;
- contradiction search;
- issue clustering;
- and institutional memory.

However, embeddings do not understand legal permission.

Therefore, retrieval tools must be governed by structured filters before semantic search.

Embedding records should include:

- source object;
- chunk ID;
- matter ID;
- client ID;
- privilege status;
- confidentiality status;
- language;
- created timestamp;
- embedding model;
- and version.

If a source object changes, embeddings may need regeneration.

## 14.22 Cost and Resource Governance

LEXOS should govern cost.

Autonomous systems can become expensive if every workflow uses high-cost models, long context windows, repeated searches, and inefficient loops.

Cost governance should include:

- model routing by task risk;
- token budgets;
- caching;
- retrieval narrowing;
- batching;
- reuse of extracted artifacts;
- confidence-based escalation;
- cost monitoring by matter;
- cost monitoring by workflow;
- and cost-to-value analytics.

However, cost control must not compromise legal reliability.

The system should spend where risk justifies it and conserve where low-cost methods are sufficient.

## 14.23 Latency Governance

Some legal tasks require speed.

Others require depth.

LEXOS should classify tasks by latency tolerance.

Examples:

Low-latency tasks:

- client status question;
- deadline alert;
- document lookup;
- simple evidence search.

High-depth tasks:

- legal research;
- adversarial review;
- argument drafting;
- cross-border analysis;
- complex evidence mapping.

The system should not use the same processing pattern for all tasks.

## 14.24 Observability

LEXOS must be observable.

Observability means the institution can see what the system is doing.

The system should track:

- workflow progress;
- agent activity;
- tool calls;
- model usage;
- costs;
- failures;
- delays;
- risks;
- pending reviews;
- unresolved contradictions;
- unsupported facts;
- output statuses;
- and external actions.

Observability supports operational control.

Without it, autonomous workflows become opaque.

## 14.25 MVP Model, Tool, and Automation Requirements

The MVP should implement a minimal but correct model/tool doctrine.

Minimum MVP requirements:

1. Record which model generated major outputs.
2. Record prompt or workflow version for major outputs.
3. Use task-specific model routing where practical.
4. Store evidence extraction outputs separately from originals.
5. Use scoped retrieval by matter.
6. Use structured outputs for core workflows.
7. Log important tool calls.
8. Track workflow status.
9. Track failures.
10. Avoid unapproved external actions.
11. Use deterministic validation where simple.
12. Track cost at least roughly by workflow or matter.
13. Maintain basic model/tool configuration records.

The MVP does not need sophisticated model-risk monitoring, full regression testing, or advanced orchestration from day one.

But it must avoid becoming an uncontrolled prompt-and-file system.

## 14.26 Target-State Model, Tool, and Automation Architecture

In target state, LEXOS should support:

- model registry;
- tool registry;
- prompt registry;
- model evaluation suites;
- drift monitoring;
- task-specific model routing;
- cost optimization;
- latency optimization;
- workflow orchestration;
- tool authority enforcement;
- audit logs;
- schema validation;
- deterministic rule engines;
- autonomous retries;
- failure queues;
- structured output enforcement;
- secure retrieval;
- and governance-aware automation.

The target-state system should be able to replace models and tools without changing the institution’s legal logic.

## 14.27 Summary of Model, Tool, and Automation Doctrine

LEXOS must treat models and tools as governed execution resources.

The governing rules are:

1. LEXOS is model-agnostic but model-aware.
2. Model output is not truth.
3. Models require approved use cases and prohibited use cases.
4. Prompts must be versioned where they affect legal work.
5. Tool access must be permissioned and logged.
6. Tool failures must create operational records.
7. Deterministic systems should be used where rules are clear.
8. AI should perform cognition, not replace basic control logic.
9. Structured outputs should be preferred for internal workflows.
10. Retrieval must be scoped before semantic search.
11. External search results must be source-graded.
12. Cost and latency must be governed.
13. Observability is required for autonomous operation.
14. MVP may be simple, but it must preserve model/tool governance foundations.

The long-term objective is to ensure that LEXOS can use changing models, tools, and automations without becoming dependent on any single component and without allowing component behavior to override institutional truth, governance, security, or legal reliability.

# Section 15 — Institutional Learning and Evolution Doctrine

## 15.1 Purpose of This Section

This section defines the institutional learning and evolution doctrine of LEXOS.

LEXOS is not intended to be a static workflow system. It is intended to become an autonomous legal institution that improves over time through experience, feedback, outcomes, adversarial testing, model evaluation, client interaction, procedural results, and accumulated institutional knowledge.

However, learning is dangerous if uncontrolled.

A legal cognition system must not simply absorb every past output, argument, correction, case result, client statement, or human preference into future behavior. Legal matters are context-specific. A strategy that works in one matter may fail in another. A successful argument may depend on facts that are not reusable. A court outcome may reflect procedural posture rather than substantive merit. A human correction may be wrong. A client preference may be strategically harmful. A model-generated improvement may be persuasive but unsupported.

Therefore, LEXOS must learn through governed institutional learning.

The central doctrine is:

> LEXOS should improve continuously, but only through structured, provenance-linked, jurisdiction-scoped, privilege-aware, outcome-sensitive, and risk-controlled learning.

The purpose of learning is not merely to make the system more efficient.

The purpose is to make the institution more accurate, strategic, resilient, defensible, and autonomous over time.

## 15.2 Institutional Learning vs Model Training

LEXOS must distinguish institutional learning from model training.

Institutional learning means that the system improves its own legal knowledge, workflows, risk controls, templates, strategies, object structures, jurisdictional profiles, and operating rules based on experience.

Model training means modifying or fine-tuning an AI model’s internal parameters or behavior.

These are different.

LEXOS does not need to train its own foundation models in order to learn institutionally.

In many cases, institutional learning should occur through:

- structured knowledge objects;
- jurisdiction profiles;
- strategy playbooks;
- risk taxonomies;
- prompt improvements;
- workflow rule updates;
- retrieval improvements;
- evaluation benchmarks;
- template refinement;
- model routing rules;
- and outcome analytics.

This distinction is important because model training may create confidentiality, privilege, cost, complexity, and control issues.

LEXOS can become much smarter through governed institutional memory even if the underlying AI models are replaced, upgraded, or externally provided.

## 15.3 What LEXOS Should Learn From

LEXOS should learn from multiple categories of experience.

Learning sources may include:

- case outcomes;
- court rulings;
- judicial comments;
- regulator responses;
- settlement outcomes;
- opposing counsel tactics;
- successful arguments;
- failed arguments;
- rejected strategies;
- client corrections;
- human lawyer revisions;
- adversarial review findings;
- citation verification failures;
- translation corrections;
- evidence extraction errors;
- workflow delays;
- procedural mistakes;
- model hallucinations;
- model performance comparisons;
- security incidents;
- privilege incidents;
- cost patterns;
- and user feedback.

However, every learning source must be classified before it becomes institutional knowledge.

An event is not automatically a lesson.

The system must determine what the event actually teaches.

## 15.4 Learning Object Doctrine

Learning should be stored as structured Learning Objects.

A Learning Object is a record of a reusable or potentially reusable insight derived from system experience.

Each Learning Object should include:

- learning ID;
- source matter or event;
- learning type;
- description;
- jurisdiction;
- workflow relevance;
- source artifacts;
- factual assumptions;
- legal assumptions;
- outcome basis;
- confidence;
- privilege status;
- confidentiality status;
- reuse permission;
- limitations;
- validation status;
- created timestamp;
- updated timestamp;
- and supersession status.

Examples of Learning Objects:

- A jurisdictional note that a particular procedural objection is often rejected unless raised before a specific stage.
- A drafting improvement showing that a certain argument structure performs better before a specific tribunal.
- A model-risk note that a certain model repeatedly misclassifies Taiwanese criminal procedure terms.
- A translation rule for a recurring legal term.
- A workflow improvement showing that evidence requests should be split into categories for better client response.
- A citation-warning pattern showing that a model frequently hallucinates cases when asked for older local jurisprudence.
- An adversarial pattern showing that a certain defense is vulnerable unless supported by bank records.

Learning Objects should be reviewed, scoped, and classified before reuse.

## 15.5 Learning Types

LEXOS should distinguish multiple learning types.

At minimum, the system should support the following categories.

## 15.5.1 Legal Doctrine Learning

Learning about substantive law, procedural law, evidence law, jurisdictional rules, or authority treatment.

Example:

- a court applies a specific test narrowly;
- a statute was amended;
- a precedent has been distinguished;
- an appellate court clarified a procedural requirement.

Legal Doctrine Learning must be jurisdiction-scoped and authority-linked.

## 15.5.2 Procedural Learning

Learning about how procedures actually operate.

Example:

- a court requires a specific format despite the rule being silent;
- a clerk rejects filings missing a particular attachment;
- a tribunal expects a certain chronology format;
- hearing schedules follow a predictable pattern.

Procedural Learning may be highly valuable but must be kept current.

## 15.5.3 Strategic Learning

Learning about strategies that worked, failed, or created unexpected consequences.

Example:

- a jurisdiction objection was persuasive when framed through corporate separateness;
- a factual attack backfired because it opened a credibility issue;
- an early evidence table improved settlement leverage;
- an aggressive tone reduced client credibility.

Strategic Learning must preserve factual and procedural context.

A strategy should not become a universal template merely because it worked once.

## 15.5.4 Evidentiary Learning

Learning about evidence collection, evidence interpretation, evidence weakness, extraction issues, or proof requirements.

Example:

- bank transfer records were insufficient without source-of-funds explanation;
- screenshots required metadata support;
- WhatsApp exports needed sender verification;
- scanned seals required vision review.

Evidentiary Learning should feed W4 and W5 improvements.

## 15.5.5 Adversarial Learning

Learning about how opponents, prosecutors, regulators, courts, or internal red-team agents attack positions.

Example:

- opposing counsel consistently attacks lack of contemporaneous documents;
- prosecutors rely heavily on company-role inference;
- judges question unsupported timeline gaps;
- regulators focus on beneficial ownership rather than formal title.

Adversarial Learning should strengthen W9 and upstream strategy.

## 15.5.6 Drafting and Rhetorical Learning

Learning about the effectiveness of drafting structure, tone, sequencing, argument framing, visuals, and bilingual presentation.

Example:

- chronological fact tables improve readability;
- overly aggressive language weakens credibility;
- issue-first headings are better than narrative headings in a specific forum;
- side-by-side Chinese/English translation reduces ambiguity.

Drafting Learning should feed W8, W10, and W11.

## 15.5.7 Translation Learning

Learning about recurring translation issues, legal terminology, ambiguity, and bilingual court expectations.

Example:

- a specific Mandarin legal term should not be translated literally in criminal-defense filings;
- a certain English business phrase is misleading if translated too strongly into Traditional Chinese;
- court-facing translations require preserving ambiguous subject structure.

Translation Learning should feed bilingual workflows, glossaries, and parallel text handling.

## 15.5.8 Model Performance Learning

Learning about model strengths, weaknesses, hallucination patterns, cost, latency, and reliability.

Example:

- a model performs well on English drafting but poorly on Chinese legal terminology;
- a model reliably extracts tables but misses handwritten annotations;
- a model tends to overstate legal confidence;
- a model generates fake citations under certain prompt conditions.

Model Performance Learning should feed the Model Registry and model-routing rules.

## 15.5.9 Workflow Learning

Learning about workflow efficiency, bottlenecks, missing gates, unnecessary steps, or failed handoffs.

Example:

- W5 repeatedly returns matters to W4 because W4 metadata is insufficient;
- clients respond better to evidence requests grouped by event;
- W7 research loops become too expensive without issue narrowing;
- W9 attacks are stronger when provided with W5 contradiction lists.

Workflow Learning should feed workflow specifications and MVP/product improvements.

## 15.5.10 Operational Learning

Learning about staffing, cost, latency, system reliability, file organization, access controls, and implementation.

Example:

- a specific parsing pipeline fails on scanned court documents;
- evidence upload errors occur when clients use phone photos;
- certain workflows need batching;
- cost spikes occur during broad legal research loops.

Operational Learning should feed technical and operations architecture.

## 15.6 Learning Scope

Every Learning Object must have scope.

Scope defines where the learning may be reused.

Possible scopes include:

- global;
- jurisdiction-specific;
- forum-specific;
- court-specific;
- judge-specific;
- matter-type-specific;
- client-specific;
- workflow-specific;
- model-specific;
- language-specific;
- evidence-type-specific;
- internal-only;
- experimental;
- deprecated.

Scope prevents overgeneralization.

For example:

A strategy that works in Taiwan criminal defense may not apply to Costa Rica civil litigation.

A drafting style that works before an administrative regulator may not suit a court pleading.

A translation convention useful for client explanation may not be suitable for court filing.

A model performance note for English contracts may not apply to Chinese criminal transcripts.

## 15.7 Learning Confidence

Learning should have confidence levels.

A single matter outcome may generate a hypothesis, not a rule.

Suggested confidence levels:

## 15.7.1 Observed

The system observed a pattern or outcome, but the lesson is not yet validated.

## 15.7.2 Plausible

The lesson appears reasonable and may be reused cautiously, but evidence remains limited.

## 15.7.3 Validated

The lesson has been confirmed by repeated outcomes, authoritative source, human/governance review, or strong evidence.

## 15.7.4 High Confidence

The lesson is strongly supported and may be used as a default rule within its scope.

## 15.7.5 Deprecated
